1.2.2 Limites et réforme de la gestion de crise Les arrêtés préfectoraux restreignant ou interdisant l'irrigation peuvent avoir un ef- [SOURCE: Page_70]

fet pénalisant important pour les agriculteurs obligés d'interrompre l'arrosage à des [SOURCE: Page_70]

périodes cruciales pour leurs cultures. Dans ce contexte de rareté, l'objectif est d'orga- [SOURCE: Page_70]

niser les restrictions de façon à maximiser la valeur sociale de l'eau. Néanmoins, dans un [SOURCE: Page_70]

contexte de crise, l'équité et l'acceptabilité des règles de partage de l'eau sont aussi des [SOURCE: Page_70]

dimensions importantes. Pour faciliter l'acceptabilité et l'e cience du rationnement, [SOURCE: Page_70]

les modalités de gestion de crise doivent être anticipables par les agriculteurs et leur [SOURCE: Page_70]

application ne doit être envisagée que lors d'épisodes climatiques exceptionnels. [SOURCE: Page_70]

Anticiper et informer les irrigants Les arrêtés sécheresse peuvent se traduire par des pertes importantes pour les agri- [SOURCE: Page_70]

culteurs quand ils ne sont pas à même de les anticiper. Reynaud (2009) évalue les [SOURCE: Page_70]

coûts induits par les arrêtés préfectoraux qui imposent des restrictions quantitatives [SOURCE: Page_70]

(apport total en eau au cours de la saison inférieur à un seuil) ou des interdictions de [SOURCE: Page_70]

prélèvements en période d'étiage. Dans le modèle, les pertes sont mesurées en terme [SOURCE: Page_70]

d'espérance d'utilité nette des coûts spéci ques à chaque système de culture. Adap- [SOURCE: Page_70]

tation de l'irrigation et réallocation des surfaces entre systèmes de culture sont deux [SOURCE: Page_70]

stratégies d'auto-assurance que les agriculteurs peuvent mettre en place contre le risque [SOURCE: Page_70]

de manque d'eau. [SOURCE: Page_70]

A court terme, le changement de stratégies d'irrigation (adaptation tactique des arro- [SOURCE: Page_70]

sages en cours de campagne d'irrigation à matériel donné) permet à l'agriculteur de [SOURCE: Page_70]

limiter l'impact sur la fonction objectif des limitations du niveau maximal d'irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_70]

A long terme, la réallocation des surfaces permet de limiter de manière très substan- [SOURCE: Page_70]

tielle l'impact des restrictions d'irrigation. La perte de fonction objectif de l'agriculteur [SOURCE: Page_70]

en cas de restriction de l'apport total en eau est de seulement 2.03% à long terme dans [SOURCE: Page_70]

l'exemple pris par Reynaud (contre 5.84% à court terme). Les interdictions d'irriguer [SOURCE: Page_70]

dans certaines périodes sont elles beaucoup plus coûteuses, avec une perte de fonction [SOURCE: Page_70]

objectif pouvant aller jusque 54% si les interdictions ne sont pas anticipées. Cependant, [SOURCE: Page_70]

l'eet négatif des interdictions d'irriguer est fortement réduit si elles sont annoncées [SOURCE: Page_70]

su samment tôt. Les agriculteurs peuvent alors anticiper et ré-optimiser leurs straté- [SOURCE: Page_70]

gies d'irrigation de manière à maximiser leur fonction objectif sous la contrainte des [SOURCE: Page_70]

interdictions d'irriguer à venir. Les simulations eectuées montrent que des interdictions [SOURCE: Page_70]

69 [SOURCE: Page_70]

d'arrosage anticipées se traduisent par une perte de fonction objectif entre 10 et 18% [SOURCE: Page_71]

selon le moment où les restrictions à venir sont annoncées. Le fait pour l'agriculteur [SOURCE: Page_71]

d'apprendre même tardivement qu'il va y avoir une interdiction d'irriguer lui permet [SOURCE: Page_71]

de limiter sa perte s'il a le temps d'irriguer avant la mise en place des interdictions. Il [SOURCE: Page_71]

est donc important pour la puissance publique d'annoncer les interdictions d'irrigation, [SOURCE: Page_71]

même tardivement. L'agriculteur peut aussi parfois modi er la surface allouée à chaque [SOURCE: Page_71]

système de culture et substituer des cultures les plus consommatrices par des cultures [SOURCE: Page_71]

plus économes en eau. Cette réallocation des surfaces entre les cultures n'est possible [SOURCE: Page_71]

que si l'interdiction d'irriguer à venir est anticipée. Par exemple en 2005 en France, [SOURCE: Page_71]

l'anticipation dès le mois de février de la réduction de la ressource en eau disponible a [SOURCE: Page_71]

conduit à la mise en place d'assolements intégrant cette perspective et à la réduction [SOURCE: Page_71]

des surfaces en maïs irrigué de 8% en moyenne nationale (Cheminaud et al. (2007)). [SOURCE: Page_71]

Les estimations de Reynaud (2009) montrent que la perte de fonction objectif peut être [SOURCE: Page_71]

limitée à 2% s'il n'y a pas de limites à la réallocation des surfaces. [SOURCE: Page_71]

A n de faciliter l'adaptation des agriculteurs, les mesures de sensibilisation, de sur- [SOURCE: Page_71]

veillance et de limitation des usages de l'eau sont prises de manière progressive à partir [SOURCE: Page_71]

de chaque franchissement de seuil. De plus, certains organismes comme les Chambres [SOURCE: Page_71]

d'Agriculture apportent leur soutien aux agriculteurs irrigants en publiant régulière- [SOURCE: Page_71]

ment un document intitulé avertissement irrigation qui donne des indications sur la [SOURCE: Page_71]

demande en eau des plantes à partir de parcelles de référence et des conseils aux agri- [SOURCE: Page_71]

culteurs pour irriguer de façon optimale leurs cultures (Henny (2010)). [SOURCE: Page_71]

Diminuer la fréquence du recours à la gestion de crise L'article 77 de la loi sur l'eau et les milieux aquatiques du 30 décembre 2006 et la [SOURCE: Page_71]

circulaire du 30 juin 2008 prévoient que les SAGE se dotent de règlements qui précisent [SOURCE: Page_71]

les volumes prélevables et la répartition entre usages sur leur territoire. L'objectif est de [SOURCE: Page_71]

revenir à des prélèvements globaux compatibles avec les ressources disponibles et ainsi [SOURCE: Page_71]

ne plus recourir systématiquement aux modalités de gestion de crise pour la gestion des [SOURCE: Page_71]

prélèvements. Le volume prélevable est le volume que le milieu est capable de fournir [SOURCE: Page_71]

dans des conditions écologiques satisfaisantes (compatible avec les principes de gestion [SOURCE: Page_71]

équilibrée de la ressource décrits dans le SDAGE). Ce volume peut être décliné par [SOURCE: Page_71]

saison et être variable en fonction d'indicateurs précis.      La loi de 2006 prévoit que [SOURCE: Page_71]

les volumes prélevables soient dé nis pour chaque ressource d'ici n 2009, ainsi que la [SOURCE: Page_71]

répartition de ces volumes entre usages. Les préfets coordonnateurs de bassins doivent [SOURCE: Page_71]

70 [SOURCE: Page_71]

proposer un programme de révision des autorisations de prélèvement pour l'irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_72]

Le volume total autorisé, qui sera la somme de toutes les autorisations des diérents [SOURCE: Page_72]

préleveurs, devra être au plus égal au volume prélevable au plus tard le 31 décembre [SOURCE: Page_72]

2014. [SOURCE: Page_72]

La loi sur l'eau de 2006 prévoit également le transfert des responsabilités en matière de [SOURCE: Page_72]

gestion des usages agricoles de l'eau à un Organisme Unique, personne morale de droit [SOURCE: Page_72]

public ou de droit privé, qui par sa désignation représente les irrigants sur un périmètre [SOURCE: Page_72]

déterminé. Les Chambres d'Agriculture, les Associations d'Irrigants, les Associations [SOURCE: Page_72]

Syndicales, les Etablissements Publics Territoriaux de Bassin, les Sociétés d'Aménage- [SOURCE: Page_72]

ment Rural etc. peuvent postuler pour devenir Organisme Unique (Erdlenbruch et al. [SOURCE: Page_72]

(2008)). Les avantages attendus de cette nouvelle institution de type guichet unique [SOURCE: Page_72]

sont la plus grande performance d'une organisation de mutualisation entre profession- [SOURCE: Page_72]

nels pour connaître les besoins de chacun, la proximité du terrain pour l'observation [SOURCE: Page_72]

des milieux, et la souplesse liée à la possibilité de transferts entre irrigants dans le cadre [SOURCE: Page_72]

d'une autorisation globale. Tous les irrigants sur un périmètre en gestion collective [SOURCE: Page_72]

doivent s'adresser à l'organisme unique pour l'attribution d'une autorisation annuelle [SOURCE: Page_72]

pour l'irrigation. Le fait d'avoir fait converger le volume total autorisé vers le volume [SOURCE: Page_72]

total prélevable devrait limiter les situations de dé cit. Néanmoins, en cas de nécessité, [SOURCE: Page_72]

le préfet pourra toujours recourir aux modalités de gestion de crise a n d'assurer le res- [SOURCE: Page_72]

pect des débits d'objectifs. Le préfet déterminera le volume de restriction à atteindre [SOURCE: Page_72]

et l'organisme unique proposera des règles pour la répartition des restrictions entre les [SOURCE: Page_72]

irrigants. La répartition des restrictions entre irrigants devra être anticipée et déter- [SOURCE: Page_72]

minée dès le projet de répartition annuelle initiale, a n de permettre une application [SOURCE: Page_72]

rapide et e ciente des restrictions. [SOURCE: Page_72]

Dans la pratique, la mise en ÷uvre d'actions de réduction des autorisations de prélève- [SOURCE: Page_72]

ment et la mise en place des organismes uniques se confrontent à plusieurs contraintes : [SOURCE: Page_72]

les réactions des agriculteurs contre la baisse des volumes prélevables, le manque de [SOURCE: Page_72]

données pour établir de façon peu contestable le niveau de la ressource disponible [SOURCE: Page_72]

sans compromettre le bon état des milieux aquatiques, la mauvaise connaissance des [SOURCE: Page_72]

prélèvements quand les services départementaux ne recueillent ou ne traitent pas sys- [SOURCE: Page_72]

tématiquement les déclarations des agriculteurs, et l'absence de candidature au titre [SOURCE: Page_72]

d'Organisme Unique dans certaines régions. Le décret de 2008 prévoyait qu'il soit mis [SOURCE: Page_72]

n à la possibilité de recourir aux autorisations de prélèvement individuelles dans les [SOURCE: Page_72]

ZRE au-delà du 31 décembre 2010 et rendait donc obligatoire l'organisation des au- [SOURCE: Page_72]

71 [SOURCE: Page_72]

torisations de prélèvement par les Organismes Uniques. Mais en raison de toutes ces [SOURCE: Page_73]

di cultés, un décret du 16 février 2011, visant à modi er le décret de 2008, a retardé [SOURCE: Page_73]

d'un an la n des autorisations individuelles : des autorisations temporaires de prélève- [SOURCE: Page_73]

ment pourront être délivrées en ZRE un an de plus, c'est à dire jusqu'au 31 décembre [SOURCE: Page_73]

2011 (et jusqu'au 31 décembre 2014 dans les zones de répartition des eaux délimitées [SOURCE: Page_73]

après le 1er janvier 2009). Ce délai a pour objectif d'attendre la mise en place eective [SOURCE: Page_73]

des organismes uniques de gestion collective. La forte sécheresse du printemps 2011 va [SOURCE: Page_73]

peut être permettre de mobiliser les acteurs pour la mise en place de cette réforme. [SOURCE: Page_73]

Alors qu'un des objectifs initiaux de la thèse était l'étude des règles de répartition de [SOURCE: Page_73]

l'eau entre irrigants mises en place par les nouveaux Organismes Uniques, le retard de [SOURCE: Page_73]

calendrier de la réforme n'a pas permis l'observation de ces règles. Néanmoins, le tra- [SOURCE: Page_73]

vail théorique et expérimental réalisé peut permettre d'informer les futurs Organismes [SOURCE: Page_73]

Uniques sur les règles les plus adéquates pour l'organisation du rationnement en cas de [SOURCE: Page_73]

sécheresse. [SOURCE: Page_73]

Les chapitres 2 et 3 s'intéressent à l'interaction entre les règles de partage utilisées [SOURCE: Page_73]

pour la gestion de crise et les stratégies d'auto-assurance des irrigants pour se protéger [SOURCE: Page_73]

contre le risque de manque d'eau. Nous nous appuyons notamment sur l'exemple du [SOURCE: Page_73]

recours aux réserves de substitution, outil de gestion du risque sécheresse de plus en [SOURCE: Page_73]

plus mobilisé par les agriculteurs français. Cette situation est traduite dans les chapitres [SOURCE: Page_73]

2 et 3 comme un jeu dans lequel les agents ont accès à une ressource en bien commun [SOURCE: Page_73]

de taille incertaine et à une ressource alternative individuelle et sécurisée. Ils font des [SOURCE: Page_73]

demandes dans les deux ressources (la demande totale étant plafonnée) avant de savoir [SOURCE: Page_73]

la taille de la ressource commune. Le régulateur dé nit la règle de partage de la ressource [SOURCE: Page_73]

commune qui sera appliquée en cas de pénurie. [SOURCE: Page_73]

72 [SOURCE: Page_73]

Chapitre 2 [SOURCE: Page_74]

Sharing rules for a Common-Pool Resource when self-insurance is available [SOURCE: Page_74]

73 [SOURCE: Page_74]

Abstract There is a risk of resource shortage if the claims agents have over a common-pool resource are incompatible with the resource size. In case of shortage and in the absence of a price mechanism, a sharing rule or bankruptcy rule is required to organize the restrictions and allocate the available resource among CPR users. However, in many situations, agents can diversify their resources and substitute an alternative safe but costly resource to the free but risky CPR. Both the sharing rule of the CPR and the resource diversi cation choices of agents contribute to optimal resource management. We nd the optimal sharing rule under two assumptions concerning the regulator: i) the social planner de nes simultaneously the sharing rule and the diversi cation choices in order to maximize social welfare; ii) the policy maker uses the sharing rule as an instrument to induce each agent to make the optimal diversi cation decision. We interpret our results in the context of water management in France. Keywords: Bankruptcy; Diversi cation; Irrigation; Self-insurance; Sharing rules; Water man- agement [SOURCE: Page_75]

Résumé Il existe un risque de pénurie sur une ressource en bien commun si la taille de la ressource est insu sante pour couvrir les demandes de tous les usagers. En cas de pénurie, en l'absence de mécanisme de prix, une règle de partage est nécessaire pour organiser le rationnement et partager la ressource disponible entre les usagers. Cependant, les usagers peuvent souvent diversi er leurs sources d'approvisionnement et substituer une ressource alternative sécurisée à l'usage de la ressource commune gratuite mais risquée. La règle de partage de la ressource en bien commun et les choix de diversi cation des agents contribuent tous deux à la gestion optimale de la ressource. Ce chapitre permet de déterminer la règle de partage optimale sous deux hypothèses distinctes concernant le régulateur : i) le plani cateur dé nit simultanément la règle de partage et les choix de diversi cations de façon à maximiser le bien être social ; ii) le décideur utilise la règle de partage comme un instrument incitant les usagers à faire des choix de diversi cation optimaux d'un point de vue social. Nous interprétons nos résultats dans le contexte de la gestion de l'eau en France. Mots clés: Faillite; Diversi cation; Irrigation; Auto-assurance; Règles de partage; Gestion de l'eau JEL Classi cation: Q25, Q58 [SOURCE: Page_75]

This paper is coauthored by Mabel Tidball (INRA), Sophie Thoyer (Montpellier Supagro) and Marc Willinger (Université Montpellier I). It has been submitted in May 2011 to Environmental and Resource Economics. [SOURCE: Page_75]

74 [SOURCE: Page_75]

2.1 Introduction Common pool resources (CPR) are often managed through the - formal or informal - [SOURCE: Page_76]

allocation of access rights or use licences to users entitled to claim a share of the resource. [SOURCE: Page_76]

When the total availability of the resource is known with certainty, optimal allocation [SOURCE: Page_76]

imposes that the marginal value of the resource to each user be equal. However, in many [SOURCE: Page_76]

cases, CPR are natural resources and quantities available can vary stochastically due [SOURCE: Page_76]

for example to climatic conditions. When the sum of claims is larger than the amount [SOURCE: Page_76]

of resource that is available, there is a need to establish a sharing rule, i.e. a rationing [SOURCE: Page_76]

scheme de ning the share of the total resource allocated to each user. An alternative [SOURCE: Page_76]

is to use a spot market mechanism that balances demand and supply at any time. In [SOURCE: Page_76]

theory, it would guarantee that available resource is allocated e ciently to the highest [SOURCE: Page_76]

value users. However, claims do rarely constitute tradable property rights and not all [SOURCE: Page_76]

resources can be traded on a spot market. Moreover, it may be costly to inform the users [SOURCE: Page_76]

in real time about the price. It will also impose on users a considerable price risk. In [SOURCE: Page_76]

the situations where spot markets are not easily implementable or desirable, balancing [SOURCE: Page_76]

variable supply and demand calls for quantity rationing (Schroyen and Oyenuga (2011)). [SOURCE: Page_76]

Under quantity rationing, users face the risk of getting a lower quantity of the CPR [SOURCE: Page_76]

than planned and claimed.       When the possibility exists, they may want to diversify [SOURCE: Page_76]

their resource use. For example they can reduce their dependence on the free but risky [SOURCE: Page_76]

CPR by investing into a costly but safer substitute resource which thereby provides an [SOURCE: Page_76]

opportunity to self-insure against resource shortage from the CPR (Ehrlich and Becker [SOURCE: Page_76]

(1972)). Agents face a trade-o between supporting the risk of CPR shortage and pay- [SOURCE: Page_76]

ing the cost of a secure resource. Since the sharing rule determines the share of resource [SOURCE: Page_76]

allocated to each agent in case of shortage, it is likely to aect agents' diversi cation [SOURCE: Page_76]

decisions.   At the society level, the optimal management of a CPR involves two di- [SOURCE: Page_76]

mensions when an alternative resource is available: (i) the rule according to which the [SOURCE: Page_76]

resource should be shared in case of shortage, and (ii) the level of risk that should be [SOURCE: Page_76]

taken at the society level, or equivalently, the level of investment in a secure substitute [SOURCE: Page_76]

resource. Suboptimal investment in self-insurance ine ciently increases the cost of risk [SOURCE: Page_76]

management (if investment is too high) or the risk of shortage (if investment is too [SOURCE: Page_76]

low). [SOURCE: Page_76]

75 [SOURCE: Page_76]

Such a situation of CPR shortage arises frequently in irrigated agriculture, when several [SOURCE: Page_77]

farmers use the same water resource for irrigation (Ambec (2008), Ansink and Weikard [SOURCE: Page_77]

(2009)). In France for instance, each farmer has to ll a form for a water use license in [SOURCE: Page_77]

October in order to be authorized to withdraw the corresponding volume from the river [SOURCE: Page_77]

and cover his irrigation needs for the coming year. The decision is made before knowing [SOURCE: Page_77]

the amount of irrigation water that will be available for the coming season and the needs [SOURCE: Page_77]

of the other farmers. In times of shortage, when water ows are too low to ful ll licences, [SOURCE: Page_77]

irrigation water is shared through a system of temporary restriction roasters, managed [SOURCE: Page_77]

and controlled by public authorities. Farmers can also partially secure their access to [SOURCE: Page_77]

irrigation water by investing into farm storage, pumping equipment for groundwater, [SOURCE: Page_77]

or by signing delivery contracts with water companies guaranteeing pressurized water [SOURCE: Page_77]

from storage dams etc.          Taking up those opportunities, they agree to forego part of 1 their claims on the river water.            Clearly, by relying more on private resources, they [SOURCE: Page_77]

relieve the collective pressure on the river and reduce the probability that total demand [SOURCE: Page_77]

exceeds total availability, mitigating as well the severity of shortage when it occurs. [SOURCE: Page_77]

From a social welfare perspective, there is a need to nd the right balance between [SOURCE: Page_77]

economic losses due to the risk of water shortage and investment costs in alternative [SOURCE: Page_77]

safe resources. Water managers must take into account the impact of the sharing rule [SOURCE: Page_77]

they implement in times of drought on the self-insurance investment decisions of farmers [SOURCE: Page_77]

in more secure water resources. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Various sharing rules are observed in the context of water management.                          The rst [SOURCE: Page_77]

innovation of the paper is to draw the parallel between these empirical rules and the [SOURCE: Page_77]

ones described in the bankruptcy literature, whereas the CPR and the bankruptcy [SOURCE: Page_77]

literature are rarely referring to one-another.             This literature provides well-behaved [SOURCE: Page_77]

and acceptable sharing rules in bankruptcy situations in order to award the remaining [SOURCE: Page_77]

resources of the rm (the estate) between its creditors (Aumann and Maschler (1985), [SOURCE: Page_77]

Herrero and Villar (2001), Moulin (2001)). Water allocation and river sharing problems [SOURCE: Page_77]

1. French farmers show a growing interest in individual reservoirs in order to diversify their water resources.   These reservoirs are called  réserves de substitution  or  retenues collinaires  in France. Some of these reservoirs are not individual but shared between a small number of neighbors.            The reservoirs are lled during winter, when water is relatively abundant and when the reservoirs' lling activity does not compete with irrigation. This resource is perceived as safe by the farmers because the quantities are known in advance (farmers can observe the quantity stored at the end of winter) and administrative restrictions do not apply to this resource. Reservoir building is assumed to have no eect on the probability of water shortage occurrence.        The investment in theses reservoirs are subsidized as part of drought management plans but farmers must hand back a share of their pumping licences corresponding to the safe volume obtained (Erdlenbruch and Montginoul (2009)) [SOURCE: Page_77]

76 [SOURCE: Page_77]

are akin to bankruptcy problems: while creditors face the risk of loosing their capital [SOURCE: Page_78]

investment when the rm's pro ts are too low, agricultural water users bear the risk [SOURCE: Page_78]

of not receiving water, whereas they have made production plans requiring water use, [SOURCE: Page_78]

when existing water rights cannot be ful lled because of low available ows (Ansink [SOURCE: Page_78]

and Weikard (2009)). [SOURCE: Page_78]

The best-known rule is the proportional rule, which recommends that awards be pro- [SOURCE: Page_78]

portional to initial claims.      The proportional rule is widely used in Australia: water [SOURCE: Page_78]

users hold water rights which give them access to a proportion of the overall amount [SOURCE: Page_78]

of seasonal water available (see chapter 5). For the case of urban water, Schroyen and [SOURCE: Page_78]

Oyenuga (2011) consider that ex ante demands are proportionally interrupted when [SOURCE: Page_78]

supply is insu cient.       The constrained equal-awards rule shares the estate equally [SOURCE: Page_78]

amongst creditors, subject to the condition that no creditor receives more than his [SOURCE: Page_78]

initial claim. A dual formulation of equality, focusing on the losses creditors incur as [SOURCE: Page_78]

opposed to what they receive, underlies the constrained equal-losses rule. It proposes to [SOURCE: Page_78]

share losses equally across creditors, subject to the condition that no creditor ends up [SOURCE: Page_78]

with a negative award. In France, water is shared through an administrative procedure [SOURCE: Page_78]

which is often implemented as a restriction of allowed irrigation times.                 For example [SOURCE: Page_78]

left bank farmers are allowed to irrigate on the rst two days of the week, and right [SOURCE: Page_78]

bank farmers are allocated the last two days... This is akin to the constrained-equal [SOURCE: Page_78]

awards rule. However, if we assume that farmers calibrate their pumping capacity on [SOURCE: Page_78]

their volumetric license, the rationing procedure is closer to the proportional rule as [SOURCE: Page_78]

the volume of water pumped during the restricted irrigation time is proportional to the [SOURCE: Page_78]

pumping capacity. It is also often the case that priority is given to group of users, for [SOURCE: Page_78]

example vegetable growers get the scarce water at the expenses of fodder producers, 2 independently of their claim over the resource (their water licences).                It is akin to the [SOURCE: Page_78]

constrained equal-awards rule but the equality condition is replaced by priority ranks. [SOURCE: Page_78]

In this paper we develop a model that allows us to study the interactions between [SOURCE: Page_78]

the rule used to share the CPR in case of shortage and users' resource diversi cation [SOURCE: Page_78]

strategies. Contrarily to most of the bankruptcy literature, the claims on the estate that [SOURCE: Page_78]

will be shared are not exogenous. The investment in the secure resource and the claim [SOURCE: Page_78]

to the CPR are dual decisions and constitute the resource diversi cation strategy of an [SOURCE: Page_78]

agent. The paper aims at determining the optimal sharing rule of the CPR under two [SOURCE: Page_78]

2. In many countries, there also exist a system of priority ranking the dierent sectors. Urban water consumption has generally the highest priority, followed by the industry. Irrigation water is generally not a priority use. [SOURCE: Page_78]

77 [SOURCE: Page_78]

dierent set of assumptions concerning the regulator: i) the regulator is a social planner, [SOURCE: Page_79]

that de nes simultaneously the optimal investment in a secure resource and the e cient [SOURCE: Page_79]

allocation of the CPR in case of shortage; ii) the regulator is a policy-maker, that can [SOURCE: Page_79]

use the sharing rule as an instrument to in uence CPR users diversi cation strategies. [SOURCE: Page_79]

We nd that the optimal sharing rule depends on social value from resource use when [SOURCE: Page_79]

the only source of heterogeneity among agents is their valuation of the resource use, [SOURCE: Page_79]

and depends on relative risk tolerance when the only source of heterogeneity among [SOURCE: Page_79]

agents is their risk tolerance.   We also determine the optimal diversi cation between [SOURCE: Page_79]

the CPR and the secure resource as a function of agents' valuation of the resource and [SOURCE: Page_79]

their risk tolerances, the distribution of the CPR size and the cost of the safe resource. [SOURCE: Page_79]

Finally, we nd that agents choose optimal diversi cation strategies when they know [SOURCE: Page_79]

the sharing rule and when this rule does not depend on individual claims. The paper [SOURCE: Page_79]

contributes to the empirical literature about the speci c issue of water rationing and [SOURCE: Page_79]

provides some advice targeted towards policy makers in charge of designing those rules [SOURCE: Page_79]

in France. [SOURCE: Page_79]

The paper is organized as follows.    Section 2.2 outlines the main assumptions of the [SOURCE: Page_79]

model. In section 2.3 we consider the case of risk neutral agents with heterogeneous [SOURCE: Page_79]

resource values. In section 2.4 we consider agents heterogeneous in their risk preferences [SOURCE: Page_79]

through a mean-variance model. Section 2.5 concludes and draws some general recom- [SOURCE: Page_79]

mendations for resource management under variable resource size when a substitute [SOURCE: Page_79]

resource is available. We illustrate and interpret our results in the speci c context of [SOURCE: Page_79]

water management in France. All the proofs are presented in the appendix. [SOURCE: Page_79]

78 [SOURCE: Page_79]

2.2 The model The model is intended to provide answers to two questions: [SOURCE: Page_80]

(Q$_1$) What is the optimal diversi cation between the CPR and the secure resource and [SOURCE: Page_80]

the optimal sharing of the resource when the total claim to the CPR exceeds the CPR [SOURCE: Page_80]

size? This rst question is related to the solution that would be chosen by a perfectly [SOURCE: Page_80]

informed benevolent social planner, who can decide both on the sharing rule and on [SOURCE: Page_80]

each individual's diversi cation strategies. [SOURCE: Page_80]

(Q$_2$) How well dierent sharing rules perform in decentralizing the optimal diversi- [SOURCE: Page_80]

cation solution?   This second question considers the more realistic issue where the [SOURCE: Page_80]

regulator uses the sharing rule as an instrument to induce CPR users to make optimal [SOURCE: Page_80]

diversi cation decisions. [SOURCE: Page_80]

2.2.1 Assumptions For clarity, the assumptions are presented with the example of irrigation water man- [SOURCE: Page_80]

agement in France. It can easily be extended to any CPR of variable size with ex-ante [SOURCE: Page_80]

claims over the resource, when a safe and costly alternative is also available for substi- [SOURCE: Page_80]

tution. [SOURCE: Page_80]

We consider a population of n = 2 farmers indexed by i = 1, 2.        The results can be [SOURCE: Page_80]

extended to the case of n agents but are more easily understandable with only two [SOURCE: Page_80]

agents. We use the masculine pronoun for the agent and the feminine for the regulator. [SOURCE: Page_80]

Each farmer has access to a maximum quantity of water           H̄i , that can interpreted as his historical water pumping right into the river, satisfying the historical needs of [SOURCE: Page_80]

water input. Since pumping rights have been largely over allocated in the past, public [SOURCE: Page_80]

authorities have decided that no additional quantities could be granted to individual [SOURCE: Page_80]

farmers. Therefore, H̄i is the maximum quantity that each farmer is allowed to draw [SOURCE: Page_80]

from the river. It is also the quantity that each farmer wishes to obtain if we consider [SOURCE: Page_80]

the marginal bene t from water use is null for any amount of water above H̄i . Each [SOURCE: Page_80]

farmer is given the choice to diversify his water sources in order to obtain the restricted [SOURCE: Page_80]

total amount of water H̄i . He has access to water from the river (CPR) and from a [SOURCE: Page_80]

private reservoir (secure resource). [SOURCE: Page_80]

The river water is an insecure resource as the total volume available from the river (size [SOURCE: Page_80]

x of the CPR) is a stochastic variable. Farmers make ex-ante claims on the river for the [SOURCE: Page_80]

79 [SOURCE: Page_80]

next irrigation season. These demands will be met if ex-post supply is su cient. Some [SOURCE: Page_81]

farmers are willing to pay to be certain to receive the quantity H̄i . The reservoir oers [SOURCE: Page_81]

a safe alternative but it entails a variable cost of c > 0 per unit of water (no xed cost). [SOURCE: Page_81]

We assume that the two resources are perfect substitutes, allowing for any combination [SOURCE: Page_81]

of claims to the two resources. We also assume that the claims to both resources are 3 realized simultaneously. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Denote by Ri farmer i's ex-ante claim on the river water.                His claim is constrained [SOURCE: Page_81]

by 0 ≤ Ri ≤ H̄i because it cannot exceed the historical water licence H̄i .                 Once the [SOURCE: Page_81]

resource size is known, in spring usually, the volume of water he can draw from the river [SOURCE: Page_81]

is denoted Qi . It may not be equal to the volume claimed if the resource size is lower [SOURCE: Page_81]

than the sum of the claims over the CPR and restrictions are organized. Since the river [SOURCE: Page_81]

and secure water resources are perfect substitutes, the investment in the safe resource [SOURCE: Page_81]

is the dual decision of the claim to the CPR: H̄i − Ri is the complementary quantity [SOURCE: Page_81]

to the secure resource. In the remainder of this paper, we assume a total use of water [SOURCE: Page_81]

equal for all farmers:     H̄i = H̄ ∀i. This enables us to study the diversi cation choices of agents according to their individual characteristics everything else being equal. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Agent i's pro t from water use is denoted Πi . It is set equal to the value of the water [SOURCE: Page_81]

units drawn from both resources (Hi = (H̄ − Ri ) + Qi with 0 ≤ Hi ≤ H̄ ) minus the [SOURCE: Page_81]

cost of the units obtained from the secure resource.             For simplicity, we assume that 4 the value of each unit of resource is constant and equal to υi .               We also assume that [SOURCE: Page_81]

the marginal value from water is always strictly larger than the cost of the alternative [SOURCE: Page_81]

resource (υi > c). [SOURCE: Page_81]

                               Πi = υi (H̄ − Ri ) + Qi (x, Ri , R−i ) − c(H̄ − Ri )                      (2.1) [SOURCE: Page_81]

3. In France, farmers le a claim form for a water use licence in winter in order to be authorized to withdraw the corresponding volume from the river during spring and summer. The decision to ll a reservoir is also taken in winter, before knowing the volume of water that will be available from the river during the irrigation season. 4. Ambec (2008) solves the problem of e cient resource allocation for a concave bene t function. The introduction of an alternative resource in the problem makes it more complex. As a result, we rely on this simplifying assumption of constant marginal value for the resource for any total quantity 0 ≤ Hi ≤ H̄ . In order to have a quasi-concave bene t function, we can consider that units of water above H̄ have a null marginal value. However, this assumption has no impact on our results. [SOURCE: Page_81]

80 [SOURCE: Page_81]

The volume of water Qi that farmer i is allowed to draw from the river depends on the [SOURCE: Page_82]

size of the resource x and on the demand for river water (R$_1$ ; R$_2$ ). For simplicity, we x−a assume that x follows a uniform distribution on the interval [a; b] (a ≥ 0): F (x) = b−a 1 and f (x) =        . Nevertheless, the results of this section can easily be generalized (in b−a implicit form) to any other distribution of x provided risk neutrality is assumed. If the [SOURCE: Page_82]

size of the resource available is larger or equal to the total claim R = R$_1$ + R$_2$ , farmer [SOURCE: Page_82]

i gets his claim Ri from the river. In the opposite case, a sharing rule is implemented. The sharing rule determines the quantity θi that agent i gets in case of restriction. As [SOURCE: Page_82]

a result, [SOURCE: Page_82]

 Q = R and H = H̄ i           i           i   if R ≤ x Q = θ and H < H̄                if R > x i       i           i [SOURCE: Page_82]

θi is only de ned for x on the interval [a; R] (when there is a shortage). There are many possible ways to de ne the relation between θi and x. The sharing rule can [SOURCE: Page_82]

be function of claims: θi (x, Ri , R−i ). The rule must satisfy "claims monotonicity" (an [SOURCE: Page_82]

agent's share of the total amount of resource can never decrease if his claim increases and [SOURCE: Page_82]

all others' claims stay constant) and resource monotonicity (if we have more resource [SOURCE: Page_82]

to distribute, no one ends up with a smaller share) (Moulin (2000)). Moreover, two other [SOURCE: Page_82]

constraints are necessary to ful ll : (C$_1$) θ1 + θ2 = x and (C$_2$) 0 ≤ θi ≤ Ri for i = 1, 2. [SOURCE: Page_82]

According to constraint (C$_1$) the sharing rule must exhaust the total quantity available [SOURCE: Page_82]

x. It means that any rule should be de ned such that the available resource x is always fully allocated when R > x. This is of course a condition for optimality as a share of 5 the resource not allocated will not contribute to social welfare.              Constraint (C$_2$) states [SOURCE: Page_82]

that no agent can get a negative quantity nor get a share of the CPR that exceeds his [SOURCE: Page_82]

claim (0 ≤ θi ≤ Ri ). This constraint relies on the assumption that an agent cannot [SOURCE: Page_82]

receive a water allocation Hi           > H̄ .      The sharing rules should be speci ed to ensure [SOURCE: Page_82]

these two conditions. Section 2.2.2 presents the dierent sharing rules mentioned in the [SOURCE: Page_82]

bankruptcy literature that we shall consider in this paper. [SOURCE: Page_82]

5. When water is not fully allocated to irrigators, the water remains into the river and contributes to environmental ows. This is of course not a pure loss for society. In the model, we consider that x is the quantity of resource that has been allocated to agricultural users such that they can use all of it without compromising the needs of other users (including the environment). [SOURCE: Page_82]

2.2.2 Bankruptcy sharing rules We follow the presentation of the bankruptcy rules of Thomson (2003) but slightly [SOURCE: Page_83]

modify these rules in order to ful ll constraints (C$_1$) and (C$_2$) de ned as the minimal [SOURCE: Page_83]

requirements for an optimal rule in our problem. Moreover, we remove the constraint [SOURCE: Page_83]

of equality and therefore modify the names: constrained-equal awards rule becomes [SOURCE: Page_83]

constrained awards rule and constrained-equal losses rule is replaced by constrained [SOURCE: Page_83]

losses rule. We can therefore de ne three sharing rules based on bankruptcy rules: [SOURCE: Page_83]

(i) The proportional rule divides the amount of resource available proportionally to the P agents' claims. In case of shortage an agent who claimed Ri receives θi         = x RRi . This rule automatically satis es constraints (C$_1$) and (C$_2$). [SOURCE: Page_83]

(ii) The CA (constrained awards) rule divides the amount of resource available among [SOURCE: Page_83]

the claimants independently from their claims. In the bankruptcy literature, the CA [SOURCE: Page_83]

rule is de ned such that an agent receives a share of the available resource θi = Ωi x Pn with     Ωi = 1 and Ωi the priority rank of each agent independent from the vector i=1 of claims (see Moulin (2000) for a characterization of priority rules). In our model, in [SOURCE: Page_83]

the case of two agents, in order to satisfy constraints (C$_1$) and (C$_2$), we rewrite the R$_2$ ≥ R      CA                CA rule as follows for Ω2 1 Ω1 : θ1  = min [Ω1 x; R$_1$ ] and θ2CA = min x − θ1CA ; R$_2$ . It guarantees that no agent receives more than his claim and the whole resource x is [SOURCE: Page_83]

allocated when x < R (Figure 2.1 ). For example, if agent 1's claim is lower than Ω1 x, [SOURCE: Page_83]

he receives his claim R$_1$ and the other agent is allocated all the resource left x − R$_1$ . [SOURCE: Page_83]

(iii) The CL (constrained losses) rule allocates the missing amount of resource.         The [SOURCE: Page_83]

claim of each agent is restricted by a share of the dierence between the aggregate claim [SOURCE: Page_83]

and the total amount available, provided no agent ends up with a negative transfer. In n P case of shortage an agent who claimed Ri receives θi = Ri − βi (R − x) with      βi = 1. i=1 For two agents, in order to satisfy constraints (C$_1$) and (C$_2$), we rewrite the CL rule as R$_2$ follows for β2 ≥ Rβ11 : θ1CL = max [R$_1$ − β1 (R − x) ; 0] and θ2CL = x − θ1CL . It guarantees that all the resource x is allocated when x < R and the awards cannot be negative. [SOURCE: Page_83]

82 [SOURCE: Page_83]

Figure 2.1: The CA rule does verify constraints C$_1$ and C$_2$ (case 2) [SOURCE: Page_84]

These three rules will be compared on the basis of their capacity to participate to the [SOURCE: Page_84]

e cient management of the CPR. We rst study whether one of these rules is an optimal [SOURCE: Page_84]

sharing rule when the regulator both xes the sharing rule and decide on the optimal [SOURCE: Page_84]

investment in the secure resource (Q$_1$). Secondly, we determine the rule which induces [SOURCE: Page_84]

farmers to take the optimal decisions in terms of diversi cation choices (Q$_2$).     The [SOURCE: Page_84]

answer to these questions will be given under dierent assumptions concerning agents' [SOURCE: Page_84]

preferences.   In section 2.3, we rst solve the model assuming risk neutral agents in [SOURCE: Page_84]

order to isolate the eect of heterogeneous values of the resource. In section 2.4, we [SOURCE: Page_84]

introduce a mean-variance objective function to observe the eect of heterogeneity in [SOURCE: Page_84]

risk tolerance when values of the resource are identical. [SOURCE: Page_84]

2.3 Risk neutral agents with heterogeneous values In this section, we assume risk neutral agents with heterogeneous values of the resource. [SOURCE: Page_85]

A risk neutral agent, with value vi of the resource, chooses his portfolio of resources in [SOURCE: Page_85]

order to maximize his expected pro t de ned as follows. We assume perfect knowledge [SOURCE: Page_85]

of υi , H̄ and the distribution of x by the two agents and the regulator. ˆ          ˆR  b                     υi  M ax E [Πi ] = (υi − c)(H̄ − Ri ) +                    Ri dx +       θi (x) dx ∀i = 1, 2       (2.2) Ri                                      b−a R             a [SOURCE: Page_85]

We rst solve the full optimization program of the regulator to answer Q$_1$. Then we [SOURCE: Page_85]

compare the rules on the basis of their capacity to decentralize the optimal claims to [SOURCE: Page_85]

the CPR (Q$_2$). [SOURCE: Page_85]

2.3.1 Social optimum: optimal sharing rule and diversi cation We assume that the social planner is a perfectly informed benevolent regulator. For- ∗                          ∗ mally, she chooses both the vector of claims to the CPR {Ri } and the sharing rule {θi } [SOURCE: Page_85]

in order to maximize the weighted sum of the expected pro ts of the two agents. λi is [SOURCE: Page_85]

the weight of agent i in the social welfare function, and λi υi measures the social value [SOURCE: Page_85]

(SVi ) of the resource used by agent i (the private value υi multiplied by the weight of [SOURCE: Page_85]

agent i λi in the social welfare function). [SOURCE: Page_85]

We consider a vast array of rules θi (x, Ri , R−i ) where the individual quantities allocated [SOURCE: Page_85]

to agent i in case of shortage may depend on the amount of resource available x and [SOURCE: Page_85]

the vector of claims (Ri , R−i ). minimum quantity of CPR available (the lower bound of the distribution            a).    It is [SOURCE: Page_87]

increasing with the cost of the alternative resource c adjusted to the value of the resource [SOURCE: Page_87]

by the lower value user. The lower-value user is decisive as the alternative resource is [SOURCE: Page_87]

relatively more costly to him. A increase in the average quantity available of the CPR [SOURCE: Page_87]

(a decrease in a and/or b) leads to a higher optimal claim to the CPR. Note that the ∗ optimal claim is independent of the total demand H̄ , as long as Ri < H̄ for i = 1, 2. [SOURCE: Page_87]

The optimal total claim to the CPR is also independent of the weights λi . The weights [SOURCE: Page_87]

only determine how this optimal total claim is shared between both agents.              When  the social planner gives a higher weight to the agent with the higher value        λ1 > λ̄ , whether the agent with the higher social value for the resource should withdraw more [SOURCE: Page_87]

or less than the other from the CPR depends on their relative values. When the social  planner gives a higher weight to the agent with the lower value       λ1 < λ̄ , there is no interior solution and the agent with the lower weight does not claim any unit to the [SOURCE: Page_87]

2.3.2 Optimal sharing rule when diversi cation is a private de- cision We assume now that the regulator cannot decide in lieu of the agents. Agents choose [SOURCE: Page_88]

their resource claims and the regulator uses the sharing rule as an instrument.     We [SOURCE: Page_88]

identify sharing rules {θi } such that agents' diversi cation choices and claims to the ∗ CPR lead to the optimal solution {Ri } found above.      Formally, the agents and the [SOURCE: Page_88]

regulator play a Stackelberg game where the regulator is the leader and the agents [SOURCE: Page_88]

the followers. The timing of the game is as follows. Step 1: the regulator announces [SOURCE: Page_88]

the sharing rule; step 2: each agent chooses his claim to the CPR so as to maximize [SOURCE: Page_88]

his expected pro t. Because the sharing rule introduces strategic interactions between [SOURCE: Page_88]

agents, we calculate the best response of each agent and identify the unique Nash [SOURCE: Page_88]

equilibrium. Knowing each agent's best response function, the regulator should choose [SOURCE: Page_88]

the sharing rule announced in step 1 such that the Nash equilibrium found in step 2 [SOURCE: Page_88]

coincides with the socially optimal claims determined in (2.5). This will ensure that [SOURCE: Page_88]

the chosen rule maximizes social welfare. The game is solved by backward induction. [SOURCE: Page_88]

The CA rule is the only rule that can decentralize the optimum as it reduces the [SOURCE: Page_89]

of shortage. However, the CA rule is not increasing with individual claim. It depends [SOURCE: Page_89]

on individual claims only through constraint (C$_2$). It therefore reduces the incentive to [SOURCE: Page_89]

claim more units to the CPR. On the contrary, the proportional and CL rules depend [SOURCE: Page_89]

CPR. The social welfare is lower under the proportional and CL rule than under the [SOURCE: Page_89]

The results of this section are rather intuitive and constitute a benchmark.       For an [SOURCE: Page_90]

irrigation basin in which farmers would mainly dier by the marginal value of water [SOURCE: Page_90]

input, the administrative rule adopted by France which consists in announcing priority [SOURCE: Page_90]

rules according to the economic weight of crops leads to less strategic interactions (and [SOURCE: Page_90]

therefore a better global use of water) than rules based on the sharing of available [SOURCE: Page_90]

water proportionally to ex-ante demands. Does such a result also hold when farmers [SOURCE: Page_90]

have identical marginal value of water but display dierent levels of risk aversion ? [SOURCE: Page_90]

In many water irrigation districts, farmers have similar cropping patterns, driven by [SOURCE: Page_90]

the soil and climate characteristics, and therefore similar marginal value for water [SOURCE: Page_90]

(driven by the market price of crops).       However, their willingness to take risk can [SOURCE: Page_90]

be dierent, for example due to their individual preferences and the nancial structure [SOURCE: Page_90]

of their exploitation. Under the assumption of risk aversion, the variability of resource [SOURCE: Page_90]

size becomes challenging as the way the resource is shared impacts the risk born by [SOURCE: Page_90]

agents and their incentive to self-insure.    Despite the large evidence of risk aversion [SOURCE: Page_90]

amongst farmers (Binswanger (1980), Couture and Reynaud (2010)...), risk preferences [SOURCE: Page_90]

are rarely taken into account in the design of mechanisms for e cient allocation of [SOURCE: Page_90]

water between heterogeneous agents (see for rare exceptions Tarlock (2000), Fapchamps [SOURCE: Page_90]

(2003), Calatrava and Garrido (2006b)). The next section deals with heterogeneity in [SOURCE: Page_90]

agents' risk tolerances. [SOURCE: Page_90]

2.4 Mean-Variance agents with equal values The model is modi ed to account for risk tolerance and heterogeneous risk preferences, [SOURCE: Page_91]

by relying on the two-moment decision model rst introduced by Markowitz (1952). [SOURCE: Page_91]

Our focus is on the consequences of risk-tolerance heterogeneity on the optimal sharing [SOURCE: Page_91]

of the resource when resource size is stochastic. Therefore, we assume equal value of [SOURCE: Page_91]

the resource (υ1 = υ2 = 1) in order to simplify the analysis. We showed that under risk [SOURCE: Page_91]

neutrality the optimal sharing of the CPR in case of shortage is such that the agent with [SOURCE: Page_91]

the higher social value for the resource receives the total available amount of resource. [SOURCE: Page_91]

We can now study the impact of heterogeneity in risk tolerance on the optimal sharing [SOURCE: Page_91]

rule and on optimal and individual diversi cation choices. [SOURCE: Page_91]

In the two-moment decision model, the risk is approximated by the variance and agents [SOURCE: Page_91]

rank choices according to their mean and their variance.       For instance, a risk-averse [SOURCE: Page_91]

agent will accept an increase in the variance of his pro t only if he receives a compen- [SOURCE: Page_91]

sation in terms of a higher mean pro t. The two-moment decision model is compatible [SOURCE: Page_91]

with any Von-Neumann Morgenstern utility function as long as the class of available [SOURCE: Page_91]

choice options is restricted to distributions that dier from each other only by location [SOURCE: Page_91]

and scale (Sinn (1983), Meyer (1987)).     For tractability and computation of explicit 2 solutions, we adopt the standard linear speci cation V (µ, σ) = γµ − ασ , where µ and [SOURCE: Page_91]

σ 2 correspond to the mean and the variance of pro t respectively. The parameter α 2 captures the agent's risk-attitude or sensitiveness with respect to σ and γ measures his [SOURCE: Page_91]

marginal utility for pro t. A positive (negative) value for α corresponds to risk-aversion [SOURCE: Page_91]

(risk-seeking) and the null value to risk-neutrality. Assuming α dierent of zero, under γ this linear speci cation, the marginal rate of substitution between pro t and risk      is α a constant. [SOURCE: Page_91]

Agents are assumed to maximize their objective function Φ [Πi ] de ned in (2.8). The [SOURCE: Page_91]

rst term is the expected pro t and the second term captures the eect of the variance 1 of pro t on the objective function. Let Ti =       be the agent's risk tolerance and set αi γ = 1 , such that Ti is also the constant marginal rate of substitution between pro t Ti and risk. We call relative risk tolerance the ratio           . We assume the two agents Ti +T−i have dierent risk tolerances. [SOURCE: Page_91]

Note that all the agents with Ri > 0 are subject to the risk on the CPR size. This risk [SOURCE: Page_92]

is exogenously given. However, the individual risk born by an agent is endogenous: it [SOURCE: Page_92]

depends on his own diversi cation choice. The more he chooses to rely on the CPR, [SOURCE: Page_92]

the higher the risk he bears.           The individual risk born by an agent is given by the [SOURCE: Page_92]

distribution of his pro t function de ned in (2.1). [SOURCE: Page_92]

The above speci cation has several advantages. Firstly, risk preferences are captured [SOURCE: Page_92]

by a single coe cient (Ti ). Secondly it takes into account three components of the cost [SOURCE: Page_92]

of risk: (i) the risk premium that agents incur due to the variability of the resource [SOURCE: Page_92]

received from the CPR; (ii) the cost c(H̄ − Ri ) of relying on the secure resource; (iii) [SOURCE: Page_92]

the opportunity cost of self insurance: under the substitution assumption, requiring a [SOURCE: Page_92]

unit from the secure resource reduces the claim on the CPR and therefore decreases the [SOURCE: Page_92]

quantity of free resource that one receive when there is no shortage. It's an opportunity [SOURCE: Page_92]

cost in the sense that the opportunity to receive free units is reduced.               Last but not [SOURCE: Page_92]

least, we get some computable analytical results for this speci cation whereas a more 7 general model is more di cult to solve. [SOURCE: Page_92]

7. Samuelson (1970) writes: Many writers have made valuable contributions the problem of optimal risk decisions by emphasizing to analyses of means and variances. These writers have realized that the results can be only approximate, but have also realized that approximate but computable results are better than none . Liu (2004) goes further: the popularity of the mean-variance analysis is possibly not because of its precision of approximating the expected utility theory but because of its simplicity and the power of its implications . The optimal sharing rule is a function of relative risk tolerance: the more risk tolerant 8 agent gets a greater share of the CPR in case of shortage.               This result can be surprising [SOURCE: Page_94]

at rst sight as one could think that a risk tolerant agent is better able to bear risk 9 and thus should be allocated less resource when it is scarce.                   However, it is optimal [SOURCE: Page_94]

that less risk tolerant agents invest more in the safe resource: the optimal claim to the [SOURCE: Page_94]

8. The optimal sharing rule is equivalent to the optimal allocation of risks in the risk sharing literature: the share of the undiversi able risk born by each agent equals the share of his own risk tolerance in the aggregate risk tolerance. This result is obtained under the assumption of expected utility maximization where risk tolerance is the inverse of risk aversion, de ned as the rate of decline of marginal utility that would occur for a one euro increase in wealth. It is not clear how we obtain a similar result under the mean-variance assumption where the risk tolerance is the trade-o parameter between these two moments. 9. Considering the optimal sharing rule of the CL form, one nd that at optimum, the agent relatively more risk tolerant sees his claim to the CPR restricted by a higher proportion:   θi = Ri − Ti Ti +T−i (R − x). This result is intuitive as it suggests that the more risk tolerant agent should bear [SOURCE: Page_94]

more of the resource shortage at optimum (which is equivalent to bearing more of the risk). However, as the more risk tolerant takes more risk at optimum, he ends up with a higher share of the available Ti resource. The rule is indeed equivalent with the CL and CA form and equal to θi = Ti +T−i x. [SOURCE: Page_94]

93 [SOURCE: Page_94]

CPR of less risk tolerant agent is eectively lower than the optimal claim of more risk [SOURCE: Page_95]

tolerant agent in (2.11). As a result, they receive a lower share of the risky resource at [SOURCE: Page_95]

optimum. [SOURCE: Page_95]

the agents. The best sharing rule is such that decentralized Nash equilibrium claims [SOURCE: Page_95]

2.5 Conclusion and policy recommendations In this paper we analyze dierent sharing rules of a risky common-pool resource when [SOURCE: Page_97]

resource-users have both ex-ante claims and the possibility to invest into a safe substi- [SOURCE: Page_97]

tute resource. [SOURCE: Page_97]

The rst innovation of the paper is to address simultaneously the issue of optimal risk [SOURCE: Page_97]

sharing and optimal risk taking. Indeed, the sharing rule used to allocate the resource [SOURCE: Page_97]

serves as a risk sharing mechanism (Eeckhoudt et al. (2005)) and the investment decision [SOURCE: Page_97]

in the secure resource is equivalent to a risk taking decision as it in uences the individual 10 risk born by an agent. [SOURCE: Page_97]

We demonstrate that the optimal investment in the safe resource for each agent depends [SOURCE: Page_97]

on his marginal social value of the resource or his relative risk tolerance, as well as on [SOURCE: Page_97]

the distribution of the CPR size and the cost of the safe resource. Relying too much on [SOURCE: Page_97]

the CPR reduces self-insurance and therefore increases individual risk. It also increases [SOURCE: Page_97]

the social risk as it increases the pressure on the CPR and therefore the frequency [SOURCE: Page_97]

and severity of shortage.         Alternatively, investing too much in the safe resource is [SOURCE: Page_97]

suboptimal since it is costly. The social optimum is used as a benchmark to compare [SOURCE: Page_97]

the performance of sharing rules when agents have the opportunity to diversify their [SOURCE: Page_97]

resource use, reduce their reliance on the CPR and therefore decide the level of shortage [SOURCE: Page_97]

risk they bear. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Our second contribution is to identify the sharing rule that a regulator should choose [SOURCE: Page_97]

in order to induce CPR users to adopt the level of diversi cation leading to a so- [SOURCE: Page_97]

cial optimum. Our model con rms the well-known result that the rules decentralizing [SOURCE: Page_97]

the optimum are rules which do not create strategic interactions between users. Any [SOURCE: Page_97]

rule sharing the resource according to individual claims (as the proportional or the [SOURCE: Page_97]

constrained-losses rules) introduces strategic interactions between agents and fails to [SOURCE: Page_97]

encourage agents to diversify optimally their resource portfolio. The constrained-awards [SOURCE: Page_97]

10. Such a risk sharing/risk taking problem has been presented by Gollier (2008) in the context of pension funds and intergenerational risk-sharing. This is one of the only paper in this branch of literature where risks are endogenous. Gollier optimizes jointly on the retirement bene t policy (risk sharing) and on the fund's asset allocation strategy between a risk-free and a risky asset (risk taking). He nds that better inter-generational risk sharing increases the risk taken by the fund and therefore the risk born by each generation. This additional collective risk exposure is only partially oset by the better risk diversi cation. Our results are unfortunately not comparable with Gollier's as the models are very dierent (one of the main dierence is that the agents face idiosyncratic risks in Gollier (2008)) but we nd interesting the similarity on the simultaneous determination of optimal risk sharing and risk taking. [SOURCE: Page_97]

96 [SOURCE: Page_97]

rule with priority parameters xed exogenously performs better. For a population of [SOURCE: Page_98]

risk-neutral agents with heterogeneous values for the resource, the priority parameter of [SOURCE: Page_98]

the constrained-awards rule should be such that the agent with the higher social value [SOURCE: Page_98]

for the resource gets all the resource available in case of shortage. When agents have [SOURCE: Page_98]

heterogeneous risk preference but identical values of the resource, the priority is given [SOURCE: Page_98]

to the agent with the higher individual relative risk tolerance. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Further work is to be done in order to design a contract such that CPR users reveal [SOURCE: Page_98]

truthfully their risk tolerance to the regulator in order to be able to implement the [SOURCE: Page_98]

optimal rule under risk aversion and asymmetric information.        We also leave for fur- [SOURCE: Page_98]

ther research numerical simulations to study the optimal sharing rule when agents are [SOURCE: Page_98]

heterogeneous both in their risk tolerance and in their value for water. An other exten- [SOURCE: Page_98]

sion could include a three-moments model where agents have a mean-variance-skewness [SOURCE: Page_98]

objective function. Recent ndings in the nance literature showed that most people [SOURCE: Page_98]

react to skewness.   This is relevant for examining the sharing rules when agents are [SOURCE: Page_98]

sensitive to left-skewed distributions (i.e.    there is a higher probability of a drought [SOURCE: Page_98]

than an excess water supply, which is more and more likely with climate change). [SOURCE: Page_98]

Our work can help a practical implementation of a scarcity sharing rule, for example in [SOURCE: Page_98]

the context of the water law reform that France is presently implementing in order to [SOURCE: Page_98]

adjust to the increased frequency of drought. A rst recommendation to policy makers is [SOURCE: Page_98]

to take into account the impact of the restrictions rule that they implement in scarcity [SOURCE: Page_98]

times on farmers choices of water resource use and investments in secure resource. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Water restriction rules which are de ned as a proportion of existing pumping rights [SOURCE: Page_98]

or licences are likely to induce sub-optimal investments in alternative safe resources [SOURCE: Page_98]

(such as reservoirs). This is an important result since many countries do rely on such [SOURCE: Page_98]

restriction rules. For example in Australia, water rights held by farmers determine their [SOURCE: Page_98]

allocation as a proportion of total water available for agriculture. In some regions in [SOURCE: Page_98]

Spain, volumes distributed in times of shortage are also calculated as proportions of the [SOURCE: Page_98]

subscribed quotas. We recommend that restrictions rules be carefully revised in order to [SOURCE: Page_98]

limit strategic interactions of farmers when claiming their annual water licences. Ideally, [SOURCE: Page_98]

the rule should take into account the relative marginal social value of water use by each [SOURCE: Page_98]

irrigator and his relative risk tolerance. A second message concerns the common practice [SOURCE: Page_98]

in France of de ning priority users in times of water restriction. Farmers growing high [SOURCE: Page_98]

value crops such as vegetables or fruit trees and contracted growers are usually exempted [SOURCE: Page_98]

from water restrictions. Our result suggests that this practice is not ine cient as long as [SOURCE: Page_98]

97 [SOURCE: Page_98]

priority ranks are independent from volumetric licences. With the increased variability [SOURCE: Page_99]

of water availability, the economic value generated by irrigation should not be the only [SOURCE: Page_99]

determinant of water allocation and the risk tolerance of the farmers should also be [SOURCE: Page_99]

taken into account. When water available for irrigation is stochastic, contracted growers [SOURCE: Page_99]

and fruit producers bear high risk on their pro ts. These farmers are therefore supposed [SOURCE: Page_99]

to be less risk tolerant.   As a result, the current practice of exemptions warranting [SOURCE: Page_99]

relatively more water to those less risk tolerant farmers in case of drought is ine cient [SOURCE: Page_99]

as it reduces their incentives to self-insure whereas it would be optimal from a social [SOURCE: Page_99]

point of view that they do so. The social cost of drought can be reduced by using an [SOURCE: Page_99]

e cient and transparent restriction rule encouraging e cient risk management decisions [SOURCE: Page_99]

at the farm level. [SOURCE: Page_99]

98 [SOURCE: Page_99]
