106 [SOURCE: Page_107]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_108]

A Common Pool Resource experiment with sharing rules and self-insurance [SOURCE: Page_108]

107 [SOURCE: Page_108]

Abstract This chapter uses laboratory experiment to study the impacts of the rules used to share a Common Pool Resource on the private decisions of resource diversi cation. Agents can self- insure against the risk of shortage by substituting an alternative safe but costly resource to the free but risky CPR. The sharing rule plays the role of a coordination device to reach the optimal level of investment in the secure resource. We compare the rules from the bankruptcy literature (contrained-equal awards, constrained-equal losses, proportional) and a rule that allocates no resource in case of shortage (traditionally used in CPR experiments). We nd that the best coordination institution is the no allocation rule but e ciency and reliability are higher with the constrained-equal awards rule. Rules which are de ned as a proportion of claims, such as the proportional and constrained-equal losses rule induce sub-optimal investments in alternative safe resources. We interpret our results in the context of water management in France. Keywords: Bankruptcy; Common-pool resource; Coordination; Experiment; Irrigation; Risk aversion; Self-insurance; Sharing rules; Water management [SOURCE: Page_109]

Résumé Cet article utilise l'économie expérimentale pour étudier l'eet de la règle de partage d'une ressource en bien commun sur les choix individuels de diversi cation, quand les usagers peuvent substituer une ressource alternative sécurisée à l'usage de la ressource commune gratuite mais risquée. La règle de partage peut faciliter la coordination pour atteindre le niveau optimal d'investissement dans la ressource sûre. Nous comparons les règles de la littérature sur les faillites (égalitaire, partage de pénurie et proportionnelle) avec une règle qui n'alloue aucune ressource en cas de pénurie (règle traditionnellement utilisée dans les expériences de ressource en bien commun). Nous trouvons que la règle qui n'alloue aucune ressource en cas de pénurie facilite la coordination mais l'e cacité et la abilité sont plus élevées avec la règle de partage égalitaire (CEA). Les règles qui tiennent compte des demandes dans la ressource commune, comme la règle proportionnelle ou la règle de partage de la pénurie (CEL), incitent au sous- investissement dans la ressource sécurisée. Nous interprétons nos résultats dans le contexte de la gestion de l'eau en France. Mots clés : Faillite ; Ressource en bien commun ; Coordination ; Expérience ; Irrigation ; Aver- sion au Risque ; Auto-assurance ; Règles de partage ; Gestion de l'eau [SOURCE: Page_109]

JEL Codes: Q28, C92, Q25 [SOURCE: Page_109]

Please note that there may be some repetitions with chapter 2. We did not try to avoid them in order to facilitate the reading article per article. Comments and suggestions are especially welcome as this article has not been submitted yet. [SOURCE: Page_109]

108 [SOURCE: Page_109]

3.1 Introduction Availability of natural resources managed as common-pool resources (CPR) is very often [SOURCE: Page_110]

variable and uncertain since it is often dependent on climatic conditions.              The stock [SOURCE: Page_110]

of natural resources available to users or the replenishment rate of renewable resource [SOURCE: Page_110]

is rarely known with certainty (Budescu et al. (1995)).            For example, surface water [SOURCE: Page_110]

available for irrigation is highly variable across seasons. When CPR users have ex-ante [SOURCE: Page_110]

claims over the resource and resource size is not su cient to cover all the individual [SOURCE: Page_110]

claims, there is a need for a rationing scheme, i.e. a sharing rule de ning the share of 1 the total resource allocated to each user. Users therefore face the risk of getting a lower [SOURCE: Page_110]

quantity of the CPR than planned and claimed. When the possibility exists, they may [SOURCE: Page_110]

want to diversify their resource use. For example they can reduce their dependence on [SOURCE: Page_110]

the free but risky CPR by investing into a costly but safer substitute resource which [SOURCE: Page_110]

thereby provides an opportunity to self-insure against resource shortage from the CPR [SOURCE: Page_110]

(Ehrlich and Becker (1972)). Agents decide individually on the level of risk they want [SOURCE: Page_110]

to bear and the cost they are willing to pay for self-insurance. However, from a social [SOURCE: Page_110]

point of view, if investment in self-insurance is too high, it ine ciently increases the [SOURCE: Page_110]

cost of risk management and if investment is too low, the risk of shortage is higher [SOURCE: Page_110]

than optimal.     Agents should coordinate in order to invest the optimal total level of [SOURCE: Page_110]

self-insurance.   Since the sharing rule determines the share of resource allocated to [SOURCE: Page_110]

each agent in case of shortage, it is likely to aect agents' diversi cation decisions and [SOURCE: Page_110]

coordination success. This paper compares the sharing rules as coordination institutions [SOURCE: Page_110]

toward the optimal level of self-insurance. [SOURCE: Page_110]

Such a situation of CPR shortage arises frequently in irrigated agriculture, when several [SOURCE: Page_110]

farmers use the same water resource for irrigation. In France for instance, each farmer [SOURCE: Page_110]

has to ll a form for a water use licence in October in order to be authorized to withdraw [SOURCE: Page_110]

the corresponding volume from the river and cover his irrigation needs for the coming [SOURCE: Page_110]

year. The decision is made before knowing the amount of irrigation water that will be [SOURCE: Page_110]

available for the coming season and the needs of the other farmers. In times of shortage, [SOURCE: Page_110]

when water ows are too low to ful ll licences, irrigation water is shared through a [SOURCE: Page_110]

system of temporary restriction roasters, managed and controlled by public authorities. [SOURCE: Page_110]

Farmers can also partially secure their access to irrigation water by investing into farm [SOURCE: Page_110]

1. A market mechanism can be used to allocate the available resource e ciently to the highest value users. However, claims do rarely constitute tradable property rights and not all resources can be traded on a spot market. [SOURCE: Page_110]

109 [SOURCE: Page_110]

storage, pumping equipment for groundwater, or by signing delivery contracts with [SOURCE: Page_111]

water companies guaranteeing pressurized water from storage dams, etc.                  Taking up [SOURCE: Page_111]

those opportunities, they agree to forego part of their claims on the river water. Clearly, [SOURCE: Page_111]

by relying more on private resources, they relieve the collective pressure on the river [SOURCE: Page_111]

and reduce the probability that total demand exceeds total availability, mitigating as [SOURCE: Page_111]

well the severity of shortage when it occurs. From a social welfare perspective, there [SOURCE: Page_111]

is a need to nd the right balance between economic losses due to the risk of water [SOURCE: Page_111]

shortage and investment costs in alternative safe resources. Water managers must take [SOURCE: Page_111]

into account the impact of the sharing rule they implement in times of drought on the [SOURCE: Page_111]

self-insurance investment decisions of farmers in more secure resources. [SOURCE: Page_111]

France is presently implementing a water law reform. One of the aspects of the reform [SOURCE: Page_111]

is the rede nition of the restriction rules in drought time.           The inherent inertia of a [SOURCE: Page_111]

social system makes extremely di cult any prediction of water users' reactions to policy [SOURCE: Page_111]

change in the water allocation scheme (Goetz et al. (2008)). Given the high cost and [SOURCE: Page_111]

imprecise measurement that confronts collection of eld data (with the implementation [SOURCE: Page_111]

of pilot experiments, for example), alternative methods are required for assessing the [SOURCE: Page_111]

performance of various sharing rules.         Experimentation provides a mean to test the [SOURCE: Page_111]

instruments at no social cost. This paper uses laboratory experiment to analyze how [SOURCE: Page_111]

the sharing rule impacts the individual trade-o between relying on a free but uncertain [SOURCE: Page_111]

CPR and investing in a secure alternative resource. [SOURCE: Page_111]

The experimental literature on CPR has mostly considered only the case where no re- [SOURCE: Page_111]

source is allocated if the resource size is not su cient to cover all the claims (thereafter [SOURCE: Page_111]

named no allocation rule) (Budescu et al. (1995)).            Alternatively, in some papers, [SOURCE: Page_111]

the scarce resource is shared proportionally to claims (Walker and Gardner (1992)). [SOURCE: Page_111]

However, in practice, various sharing rules are observed in the context of water man- [SOURCE: Page_111]

agement.    These empirical rules are similar to the ones described in the bankruptcy [SOURCE: Page_111]

literature, that is providing well-behaved and acceptable sharing rules in bankruptcy [SOURCE: Page_111]

situations in order to award the remaining resources of the rm (the estate) between its 2 creditors (Aumann and Maschler (1985), Herrero and Villar (2001), Moulin (2001)). [SOURCE: Page_111]

Water allocation and river sharing problems are akin to bankruptcy problems: while [SOURCE: Page_111]

creditors face the risk of loosing their capital investment when the rm's pro ts are [SOURCE: Page_111]

too low, agricultural water users bear the risk of not receiving water, whereas they [SOURCE: Page_111]

2. These rules have in common that they are the only three rules that simultaneously satisfy an intuitively reasonable set of axioms: equal treatment of equals, scale invariance, composition, path- independence and consistency [SOURCE: Page_111]

110 [SOURCE: Page_111]

have made production plans requiring water use, when existing water rights cannot be [SOURCE: Page_112]

ful lled because of low available ows (Ansink and Weikard (2009)). [SOURCE: Page_112]

The best-known rule is the       proportional rule, which recommends that awards be pro- portional to initial claims.     The proportional rule is widely used in Australia: water [SOURCE: Page_112]

users hold water rights which give them access to a proportion of the overall amount of [SOURCE: Page_112]

seasonal water available. The       constrained equal-awards rule shares the estate equally amongst creditors, subject to the condition that no creditor receives more than his [SOURCE: Page_112]

initial claim. A dual formulation of equality, focusing on the losses creditors incur as [SOURCE: Page_112]

opposed to what they receive, underlies the        constrained equal-losses rule. It proposes to share losses equally across creditors, subject to the condition that no creditor ends up [SOURCE: Page_112]

with a negative award. In France, water is shared through an administrative procedure [SOURCE: Page_112]

which is often implemented as a restriction of allowed irrigation times.               For example [SOURCE: Page_112]

left bank farmers are allowed to irrigate on the rst two days of the week, and right [SOURCE: Page_112]

bank farmers are allocated the last two days... This is akin to the constrained-equal [SOURCE: Page_112]

awards rule. However, if we assume that farmers calibrate their pumping capacity on [SOURCE: Page_112]

their volumetric licence, the rationing procedure is closer to the proportional rule as [SOURCE: Page_112]

the volume of water pumped during the restricted irrigation time is proportional to the [SOURCE: Page_112]

pumping capacity. [SOURCE: Page_112]

The experimental literature on bankruptcy issues is fairly new. Two experimental pa- [SOURCE: Page_112]

pers have studied how subjects reach an agreement on bankruptcy problems in the lab 3 (Gachter and Riedl (2006), Herrero et al. (2010)). The focus of these papers is how [SOURCE: Page_112]

to cope with bankruptcy after it has occured (ex-post). Claims are exogenously given [SOURCE: Page_112]

in the experiment and there is no mention of the potential ex-ante impact of the rule [SOURCE: Page_112]

on the claims. On the contrary, Di Cagno and Spallone (2010) test experimentally the [SOURCE: Page_112]

eectiveness of a mechanism designed to be an optimal bankruptcy law both before [SOURCE: Page_112]

(ex-ante) and after (ex-post) a nancial distress.          Ex-post e ciency implies that re- [SOURCE: Page_112]

maining assets are directed toward their highest valued use. Ex-ante e ciency implies [SOURCE: Page_112]

that entrepreneurs realize an optimal amount of eort into the rms.                  Their experi- [SOURCE: Page_112]

ment measures the level of eort induced by two mechanisms that can be compared [SOURCE: Page_112]

3. These papers consider a situation where the authority in charge of solving a bankruptcy problem resort to a non cooperative procedure to choose the sharing rule. Each subject is assigned to a player position, corresponding to a particular claim in the bankruptcy problem (given exogenously). In each round, the subjects choose simultaneously a rule among three possible rules. Individual choices are transformed into a collective one by a voting procedure. Round payos (losses) are determined by the dierence between their claims and the share of the available resource assigned to them according to the rule chosen. [SOURCE: Page_112]

111 [SOURCE: Page_112]

to bankruptcy rules. The experiment presented in this paper is a mix as it compares [SOURCE: Page_113]

the dierent rules mentioned above on the basis of their impact on the ex-ante claims [SOURCE: Page_113]

of the agents. Rules are exogenously given (treatment variables) and the claims to the [SOURCE: Page_113]

CPR are endogenous and constitute the decision task of each agent. [SOURCE: Page_113]

We compare the rules on the basis of their e ciency (total gains) and reliability (vari- [SOURCE: Page_113]

ability of total gains). We also study the impact of the rules on the coordination level. [SOURCE: Page_113]

We rank the instruments according to these dierent criteria. In light of the  collective [SOURCE: Page_113]

action nature of the problem (Coman (1911), Ostrom (2011)), it's useful to identify the [SOURCE: Page_113]

most e cient coordination institution among the restriction systems or sharing rules. [SOURCE: Page_113]

We nd that coordination success is higher with the no allocation rule but e ciency and [SOURCE: Page_113]

reliability are higher with the constrained-equal awards rule. Rules which are de ned as [SOURCE: Page_113]

a proportion of claims, such as the proportional and the constrained-equal losses rules, [SOURCE: Page_113]

induce sub-optimal investments in alternative safe resources, therefore lower gains, and [SOURCE: Page_113]

are less reliable. We interpret our results in the context of water management in France. [SOURCE: Page_113]

The paper is organized as follows. Section 3.2 describes the experimental design. Sec- [SOURCE: Page_113]

tion 3.3 summarizes the theoretical predictions. Results are presented in section 3.4. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Section 3.5 concludes. [SOURCE: Page_113]

112 [SOURCE: Page_113]

3.2 Experimental design 3.2.1 Description of the game The experiment is based on a symmetric 2-players CPR game. In a traditional CPR [SOURCE: Page_114]

game, subjects receive an initial endowment and can decide to invest a share of this en- [SOURCE: Page_114]

dowment in a CPR. The remaining share is kept by the subject. The game is presented [SOURCE: Page_114]

dierently in this experiment in order to represent the situation of irrigation water use [SOURCE: Page_114]

presented above. Each subject has to claim a total of 20 units and can allocate this total [SOURCE: Page_114]

demand between a CPR of uncertain size (denoted account B) and a safe alternative [SOURCE: Page_114]

(denoted account A). [SOURCE: Page_114]

The units claimed are not received systematically. Each unit received yields a payo 4 of 1 Euro (payos are given directly in Euros; no experimental currency is used).                     All [SOURCE: Page_114]

the units claimed to account A are received with certainty but it is required to pay a [SOURCE: Page_114]

per-unit cost of 0.2 Euros .       Because of environmental and strategic uncertainty, the [SOURCE: Page_114]

number of units received from account B can be lower than the number of units claimed [SOURCE: Page_114]

to B. The total number of units available from account B is randomly drawn at the [SOURCE: Page_114]

end of the experiment and can take two values:             l = 12 or h = 40 units. 5 The number of units received from account B by a subject depends both on his claim to B, the [SOURCE: Page_114]

aggregate claim to B and the random draw of the total number of units. If the resource [SOURCE: Page_114]

size is compatible with the sum of claims, each subject receives his claim. When total [SOURCE: Page_114]

claim is greater than the number of units available, rationing is organized according [SOURCE: Page_114]

to the sharing rule that determines which share of the total number units available is [SOURCE: Page_114]

received by each subject. Both low resource size and high claims can create a situation [SOURCE: Page_114]

where rationing is necessary. [SOURCE: Page_114]

4. We have chosen to have a linear utility function in order to limit the complexity of the experiment. Some of the rules are complex enough not to add additional complexity with a concave utility function and decreasing marginal value of each unit of water received. The disadvantage of this choice is that it attens the gain function. The design is therefore subject to the  at maximum criticism (Harrison (1989)), according to which deviations from the Nash equilibrium may be caused by low monetary incentives since the costs of such deviations are often rather small. 5. The assumption on the resource size is modi ed compared to the theoretical model of chapter 2 where the resource size is distributed uniformly between a and b.     This assumption simpli es the experimental design and reduces the cognitive demand to the subjects.        Moreover, the theoretical predictions under this assumption can more easily be tested. The theoretical predictions presented in section 3.3 show that all rules lead to the same total claim to the CPR at equilibrium. Any dierence between the sharing rules observed with the experimental data will therefore challenge this theoretical prediction. [SOURCE: Page_114]

113 [SOURCE: Page_114]

The subjects face the following trade-o: claiming units to account B yields a better [SOURCE: Page_115]

payo if one receives his claim from account B (because the units claimed to B are [SOURCE: Page_115]

free); On the other hand, increasing the number of units claimed to B reduces the [SOURCE: Page_115]

likelihood that these units are received (because it increases the probability and severity [SOURCE: Page_115]

of shortage). In case of rationing, the opportunity cost of each unit claimed to B but [SOURCE: Page_115]

not received is equal to 0.8 Euros. This corresponds to the gain that would have been [SOURCE: Page_115]

obtained by claiming this unit to account A minus the cost (1-0.2 Euros per unit). [SOURCE: Page_115]

This design relaxes two features of the original design of CPR games. In early CPR [SOURCE: Page_115]

games with uncertain resource size (Budescu et al. (1995)), a zero claim to the CPR [SOURCE: Page_115]

yields a zero payo. Moreover, if the total claim exceeds the resource size, subjects get [SOURCE: Page_115]

nothing; otherwise they get their claim. The underlying reason is that it is assumed [SOURCE: Page_115]

that the resource is destroyed when total claim is greater than resource size (therefore [SOURCE: Page_115]

no resource can be allocated).    The rst dierence we introduce is the existence of [SOURCE: Page_115]

a substitute resource to the CPR. All the units that are not claimed to the CPR [SOURCE: Page_115]

must be claimed to the alternative resource.      As a result, one can receive a payo [SOURCE: Page_115]

without using the CPR. Resource diversi cation was previously introduced in CPR [SOURCE: Page_115]

games by Walker and Gardner (1992) and Keser and Gardner (1999). In their settings, [SOURCE: Page_115]

each appropriator has an endowment of resource which can be invested in the CPR or [SOURCE: Page_115]

invested in a safe outside activity. The second dierence concerns the outcome in case [SOURCE: Page_115]

of resource shortage. Whereas early CPR experiments have considered the case where [SOURCE: Page_115]

the resource is destroyed in case of over-lapping claims, in many real-world examples [SOURCE: Page_115]

(as in the example of water management), access to the resource is not prohibited but [SOURCE: Page_115]

only restricted according to a pre-de ned sharing rule. The complete interdiction to use [SOURCE: Page_115]

the resource available does not satisfy the acceptability criteria. In Walker and Gardner [SOURCE: Page_115]

(1992), the resource is shared proportionally (more precisely, the payo from the CPR [SOURCE: Page_115]

depends on aggregate investment into the CPR and on the appropriator investment as [SOURCE: Page_115]

a percentage of the aggregate). This experiment enables to explore this idea further [SOURCE: Page_115]

and study the impact of dierent sharing rules on CPR use. [SOURCE: Page_115]

In a between subject design, we compare the traditional rule of no resource allocation [SOURCE: Page_115]

(NA) with three sharing rules that have been studied in the bankruptcy literature: [SOURCE: Page_115]

the proportional rule (PROP) , the constrained equal-awards rule (CEA) and the con- [SOURCE: Page_115]

strained equal-losses rule (CEL). These bankruptcy rules are expected to increase total [SOURCE: Page_115]

gains compare to the no allocation rule as they make some resource available to users [SOURCE: Page_115]

even when the resource size is insu cient to fully cover existing claims. On the other [SOURCE: Page_115]

114 [SOURCE: Page_115]

hand, the no allocation rule increases the losses due to coordination failure and there- [SOURCE: Page_116]

fore creates stronger incentives to coordinate. As a result, a better coordination with [SOURCE: Page_116]

the no allocation rule could lead to higher total gains. [SOURCE: Page_116]

3.2.2 Experimental procedure The experiment was conducted at the University of Montpellier experimental lab (LEEM) [SOURCE: Page_116]

with a total of 156 subjects drawn from the undergraduate student population. We have [SOURCE: Page_116]

conducted 2 sessions for each treatment with 10 pairs in each session, resulting in 20 [SOURCE: Page_116]

independent observations per treatment (there are less subjects in one session, see   Ta- ble 3.1 for details). Care was taken to ensure that no subject participate in more than one session. Common knowledge of the rules of the game was implemented by reading [SOURCE: Page_116]

aloud the instructions (see Appendix 3.B). [SOURCE: Page_116]

Table 3.1: Experimental Design [SOURCE: Page_116]

Rule     Stages Order    Number of subjects     Number of periods (CPR game) 1    NA        R-S-CPR                  20                   10 2   CEA        R-S-CPR                  20                   20 3   PROP       R-S-CPR                  20                   20 4   CEL        R-S-CPR                  20                   10 5   CEL        R-S-CPR                  16                   20 6    NA        S-R-CPR                  20                   20 7   CEA        S-R-CPR                  20                   20 8   PROP       S-R-CPR                  20                   20 R: risk aversion elicitation task S: strategic uncertainty elicitation task CPR: main task (CPR game) [SOURCE: Page_116]

Subjects were randomly divided into xed pairs for the duration of the experiment. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Interactions were restricted to simultaneous decisions via computer terminals. No com- [SOURCE: Page_116]

munication between subjects was allowed. The game was repeated 20 periods by the [SOURCE: Page_116]

same pair of 2 players (two sessions have only 10 periods).       In order to avoid hedg- [SOURCE: Page_116]

ing behaviors and end-of-the game eect, we told the subjects that the game will be [SOURCE: Page_116]

repeated but we did not tell them the number of periods. We simply informed them [SOURCE: Page_116]

when it was the end of the experiment. The players had complete information about [SOURCE: Page_117]

the payo function for each player and the values of all parameters. In the beginning [SOURCE: Page_117]

of each period, the 2 players simultaneously and independently made their decisions. [SOURCE: Page_117]

We have elicited beliefs by asking subjects to declare the number of units they expect [SOURCE: Page_117]

the other player to claim to B. At the end of each period, each player learned the [SOURCE: Page_117]

dierence between his prediction bˆ −i and the claim of the other player to the CPR [SOURCE: Page_117]

b−i , as well as his gain from this prediction. Beliefs elicitation allows to examine the source of deviations from equilibrium behavior and help distinguish between failure [SOURCE: Page_117]

to best respond to beliefs and failure to form accurate beliefs.                        Belief elicitation was [SOURCE: Page_117]

incentivized with a scoring rule de ned such that the closer a subject's stated belief from 6 the actual claim, the higher his payo (Croson (2000), Gachter and Renner (2010)). [SOURCE: Page_117]

 prediction Y                  20 Euros         if bˆ−i = b−i = i  |b−i 18 ˆ −b−i | Euros   if bˆ−i 6= b−i [SOURCE: Page_117]

At any time during the game, subjects could use a simulator to compute their gains [SOURCE: Page_117]

in each state of nature. They needed to enter their claim and their belief about the [SOURCE: Page_117]

other's claim in the simulator. They could also look at the history of the game. [SOURCE: Page_117]

At the end of each period, each player was informed about the total claim to the CPR [SOURCE: Page_117]

and about his gains in the two possible states of nature (12 or 40 units available from 7 the CPR). We did not reveal the gains in each period in order to avoid wealth eects. [SOURCE: Page_117]

The draw of the state of nature (12 or 40) is realized only at the end of the experiment. [SOURCE: Page_117]

Moreover, we used a random lottery choice payment procedure, where we randomly [SOURCE: Page_117]

payed for a single situation (for one round): either for the accuracy of subjects' stated 8 beliefs or the payo associated with the decision task. . The random-lottery incentive [SOURCE: Page_117]

6. Several studies indicate that subjects report their true beliefs when incentivized by the Quadratic Scoring Rule (Sonnemans and Oerman (2001), Nyarko and Schotter (2002)). This function is designed such that it is optimal for a risk neutral player to report his true belief. The scoring rule we used is not proper in the sense that it is not incentive compatible for stating the true belief. However, our primary interest in this paper is not whether beliefs are truthful. We have preferred to use a simple scoring rule rather than confusing subjects and shifting their attention from the main decision task to the belief elicitation task. 7. There is a potential wealth eect if subjects modify their decisions according to the gains already obtained (for example by taking more or less risk than in the previous periods). 8. We are aware that subjects asked to state beliefs may have dierent behaviors in the main decision task. There have been some experimental evidence of incentivized belied elicitation modifying the observed behaviors in public good and coordination games (Croson (2000), Gachter and Renner [SOURCE: Page_117]

116 [SOURCE: Page_117]

system has become the almost exclusive incentive system for individual choice, and [SOURCE: Page_118]

numerous studies have used and tested it (Starmer and Sugden (1991), Hey and Lee [SOURCE: Page_118]

(2005), Lee (2008)). This gives us the highest possible impact of risk aversion on any [SOURCE: Page_118]

decision as stakes in Euros are higher for each decision (Heinemann et al. (2004)). [SOURCE: Page_118]

Prior to the CPR game, we elicited two types of risk preferences: exogenous risk aversion [SOURCE: Page_118]

and aversion to strategic uncertainty. Risk aversion has been shown to have signi cant [SOURCE: Page_118]

impact on decisions in CPR game (Budescu et al. (1995), Chermak and Krause (2002)) 9 as well as in coordination game (Heinemann et al. (2009)). We elicit risk aversion [SOURCE: Page_118]

in a lottery-choice task to capture the sensitivity of subjects to exogenous risk. The [SOURCE: Page_118]

more risk averse a subject, the less willing he is to choose the option that requires [SOURCE: Page_118]

coordination, as the other can be considered as a source of uncertainty. We have also [SOURCE: Page_118]

elicited subjects' aversion to strategic uncertainty with a coordination game framed in [SOURCE: Page_118]

a similar way as the risk aversion elicitation task (see Bohnet and Zeckhauser (2004) [SOURCE: Page_118]

and Heinemann et al. (2009) for similar procedures).             We controlled for order eects [SOURCE: Page_118]

by having sessions where the risk task was run before the strategic task (RS) and vice [SOURCE: Page_118]

versa (SR) (   Table 3.1 ). Our elicitation method is derived from the investment game (Gneezy and Potters (1997), [SOURCE: Page_118]

Charness and Gneezy (2009)). In a investment game, subjects choose how to allocate [SOURCE: Page_118]

their initial endowment between a sure asset and a risky asset. Clearly, the higher the [SOURCE: Page_118]

investment in the risky asset, the less risk averse the subject. For coherence with respect [SOURCE: Page_118]

to the decision task, we framed the elicitation tasks in the same terms as the CPR game: 10 account A is the sure asset and account B the risky asset.             The subject always gets the [SOURCE: Page_118]

units claimed to account A but have to pay 0.2 Euros per unit claimed to account A. [SOURCE: Page_118]

On the contrary, it is free to claim units from option B but the number of units received [SOURCE: Page_118]

from option B is uncertain. In the exogenous risk aversion elicitation task, the number [SOURCE: Page_118]

(2010), Blanco et al. (2010)). Nevertheless, we expect this eect of belief elicitation to be constant across treatment, thus not impacting the comparison of the dierent sharing rules. Moreover, paying randomly either for the accuracy of subjects' stated beliefs or pay the payo associated with the game outcome makes hedging no longer possible (Blanco et al. (2010)). Indeed, high earnings in the tasks related to the game studied cannot compensate for low earnings in the belief elicitation task, or vice versa. We keep overall incentives per task the same in expected terms. 9. Devetag and Ortmann (2007) write that the eects of risk attitudes on the emergence of coordi- nation successes have hardly been studied. Early attempts by Cooper et al. (1990, 1992) to control for risk preferences through the Roth and Malouf (1979) procedure in the stag hunt game were laudable but problematic. 10. Wilcox (2009) shows that when elicitation task context and target task context dier, there is no guarantee that risk attitudes measured in the two tasks will be consistent unless very speci c estimation techniques are used. [SOURCE: Page_118]

of units received from account B depended on the ip of a coin: if head is drawn, one [SOURCE: Page_119]

receives twice the units claimed to account B; if tail is drawn, one receives 0 unit from [SOURCE: Page_119]

account B. We used the number of units claimed to the safe option (A) as a proxy [SOURCE: Page_119]

measure of risk aversion (aversion against participating in a lottery against nature). In [SOURCE: Page_119]

the strategic uncertainty aversion elicitation task, the number of units received from [SOURCE: Page_119]

account B depends on the total claim to option B, hence on the other's decision: if the [SOURCE: Page_119]

total claim to option B is lower or equal to 20 units, one receives two times the units [SOURCE: Page_119]

claimed to account B; if the total claim is strictly more than 20 units, one receives 0 [SOURCE: Page_119]

units from account B. Subjects therefore had to coordinate on a total claim of 20 units [SOURCE: Page_119]

to account B in order to maximize their joint gains. We use the number of units claimed [SOURCE: Page_119]

to the safe option (A) as a measure of strategic uncertainty aversion (aversion against [SOURCE: Page_119]

participating in a coordination game). The decisions in the risk aversion and strategic [SOURCE: Page_119]

uncertainty aversion elicitation tasks are two of the 22 decisions that can be selected for [SOURCE: Page_119]

payment at the end of the experiment.     Table 3.4 (presented with other tables at the end of the chapter) and   Figure 3.1 present the results of the two preference elicitation tasks. [SOURCE: Page_119]

118 [SOURCE: Page_119]

Figure 3.1: Distribution of Risk and Strategic Uncertainty Aversion [SOURCE: Page_120]

This histogram represents the distribution of the individual claims to the safe account (A) -in frequency-.Each bar represents the proportion of subjects that have claimed the corresponding number of units to A. We nd a signi cant order eect for the risk aversion task: subjects are signi cantly more risk averse if they play the risk task rst (two sided Mann Whitney test p value=0.026). There is no signi cant order eect for the strategic task (two sided Mann Whitney test p value=0.857). We therefore present the results of preference elicitation separately for the two orders. In average, claim to A is not signi cantly dierent in the two tasks, thus suggesting that subjects are neither more nor less willing to play against nature than to depend on another subject's behavior (Wilcoxon paired test p-value=0.45 for order RS, p-value=0.12 for SR - we have similar results with paired t test). We nd that the claims in the risk task and strategic task are signi cantly and positively correlated in the two sub-samples (Spearman= 0.22, pvalue=0.013 for the RS subsample and Spearman= 0.17, pvalue=0.08 for the SR subsample). One observes more variation in the responses to the risk aversion elicitation task than in the strategic uncertainty task. [SOURCE: Page_120]

3.3 Theoretical predictions Assume 2 agents, who both have to allocate a total demand of 20 units between two [SOURCE: Page_121]

resources. The individual claim of agent i to the CPR resource (account B) is denoted [SOURCE: Page_121]

bi , with 0 ≤ bi ≤ 20. In the experiment, the claim decision is a discrete choice (integer between 0 and 20) but we solve the equilibrium and optimum assuming the choice is [SOURCE: Page_121]

continuous. The claim to the safe resource (account A) is the complementary decision: [SOURCE: Page_121]

20 − bi . The cost of each unit claimed to the safe resource is c per unit. Denote x the total number of units available from account B. x takes two values l and h [SOURCE: Page_121]

with equal probability (l < h). The vector of claims on account B is denoted b = (b^1 , b^2 ) [SOURCE: Page_121]

and the total claim B = b^1 + b^2 . If B ≤ x, each agent receives the number of units [SOURCE: Page_121]

claimed. If B > x, a bankruptcy or claim problem occurs in the CPR for a pair (b, x). [SOURCE: Page_121]

A solution to the claims problem is a function θ(b, x), a rule that associates with each [SOURCE: Page_121]

problem in the class a division of the estate x between the claimants.                                 θi (b, x) is the amount of CPR allocated to agent i. [SOURCE: Page_121]

We consider the special case where the size of the resource is su cient to satisfy demand [SOURCE: Page_121]

in the state of nature h whatever the total claim to B (h = 40). As a result, each player [SOURCE: Page_121]

receives his claim to B when the state of nature is h and the sharing rule θ(b, l) applies [SOURCE: Page_121]

only when the state of nature is l and B                > l. The objective function of each agent i = 1, 2 is equal to the expected payo given by: [SOURCE: Page_121]

1                         1 Q                                   Q                         Q E[       i ] (bi , b−i )     =       2       i,h (bi , b−i ) + 2       i,l (bi , b−i ) [SOURCE: Page_121]

Q with        i,h (bi , b−i )   =        (1 − c).(20 − bi ) + bi                                     (3.1) (1 − c).(20 − b ) + θ (b, l) if B > l Q                                           i      i i,l (bi , b−i )       = (1 − c).(20 − b ) + b          if B ≤ l i      i [SOURCE: Page_121]

We consider sharing rules that have the following properties in order to guarantee that [SOURCE: Page_121]

no agent receives more than his claim or a negative number of units, and the total [SOURCE: Page_121]

amount of resource is allocated in case of shortage (B > l) [SOURCE: Page_121]

Maximizing (3.2) over B , one nds that the optimum solution is a total claim to the 1 CPR equal to l (provided c <                            ).     Any division of this total claim between the two 2 agents is optimal. This result stands for any sharing rule θ . [SOURCE: Page_126]

Note that the aggregate Nash equilibrium is equal to the optimal total claim. In other [SOURCE: Page_126]

words, coordination on the lowest quantity available from account B (l ) is maximiz- [SOURCE: Page_126]

ing both total and individual gains. However, agents face a coordination problem as [SOURCE: Page_126]

each player must make a speci c choice that is valuable only if the other player makes [SOURCE: Page_126]

the complementary selection.Multiple equilibria increase strategic uncertainty and the [SOURCE: Page_126]

probability of a coordination failure (Van Huyck et al. (1990)). The unicity of Nash [SOURCE: Page_126]

equilibrium for the CEA rule is expected to facilitate coordination, whereas the three [SOURCE: Page_126]

other rules have multiple equilibria.                           However, the cost of deviation from Nash equi- [SOURCE: Page_126]

librium is higher with the NA rule as no resource is allocated with probability one [SOURCE: Page_126]

half if total claim is higher than l . This rule is therefore also expected to lead to high [SOURCE: Page_126]

coordination levels.            Figure 3.2 shows the total gains according to the total claim and the sharing rule. It clearly shows that deviation from a total claim of 12 units is more [SOURCE: Page_126]

costly in terms of total gains with the NA rule than with the three bankruptcy rules. [SOURCE: Page_126]

Along with coordination failure, a second concern of coordination games is which equi- [SOURCE: Page_126]

librium arises. When games have multiple Nash equilibria, this is problematic from a [SOURCE: Page_126]

forecasting perspective. What factors of the game determine which is the reasonable or [SOURCE: Page_126]

plausible equilibrium? In this game, agents cannot agree on a ranking of the possible [SOURCE: Page_126]

coordination outcomes: there is no Pareto-dominant equilibrium among all the possible [SOURCE: Page_126]

Nash equilibria. Moving from one equilibrium to the other, one player has higher gains [SOURCE: Page_126]

and the other has lower gains, the total gains being constant. [SOURCE: Page_126]

Figure 3.2: Cost of Deviation from Total Optimal Claim [SOURCE: Page_127]

The concept of risk dominance enables to select the equilibrium that minimizes the risk [SOURCE: Page_127]

for each player.   The least risky decision is the one that has the greatest number of [SOURCE: Page_127]

possible strategies resulting in it being the best response. We apply the de nition of [SOURCE: Page_127]

risk-dominance based on Nash products given by Harsanyi and Selten (1988) to deter- [SOURCE: Page_127]

mine which of the multiple Nash equilibria are risk dominant: an equilibrium is risk [SOURCE: Page_127]

dominant if the Nash Product (product of the surpluses generated through success- [SOURCE: Page_127]

ful coordination) of that equilibrium is greater than the Nash Product of every other [SOURCE: Page_127]

equilibrium. The risk dominant equilibrium is (b^1 ; b^2 ) = (6; 6) for the PROP and the [SOURCE: Page_127]

NA rule. However, for the CEL rule, there are 7 risk-minimizing equilibria among the [SOURCE: Page_127]

13 equilibria -(b^1 ; b^2 ) = (9; 3)(8; 4)(7; 5)(6; 6)(5; 7)(4; 8)(3; 9)-. In order to discriminate [SOURCE: Page_127]

among these equilibria, one has to use the concept of focal point introduced by Schelling [SOURCE: Page_127]

(1960). A focal point represents a natural outcome for the game. If participants believe [SOURCE: Page_127]

that others will focus on one of the Nash equilibria, it makes it the outcome of their [SOURCE: Page_127]

interaction. The issue of selection then reduces to a search for a "natural" focal point [SOURCE: Page_127]

for the game. One obvious arrangement, if subjects are averse to inequality, is sharing [SOURCE: Page_127]

equally the total claim among the two participants (b^1 ; b^2 ) = (6; 6). This is the unique [SOURCE: Page_127]

pure strategy symmetric equilibrium. As a result, one expect the equilibrium (6; 6) to [SOURCE: Page_127]

be the more frequent for each rule: it's the unique Nash equilibrium for the CEA rule, [SOURCE: Page_127]

it is the risk dominant equilibrium for the PROP and the NA rule, and an obvious focal [SOURCE: Page_127]

point for the CEL rule. [SOURCE: Page_127]

126 [SOURCE: Page_127]

The use of the Nash equilibrium concept to predict individual behaviors has been chal- [SOURCE: Page_128]

lenged by experimental data in CPR games (Walker and Gardner (1992), Keser and [SOURCE: Page_128]

Gardner (1999)) as well as coordination games (Heinemann et al. (2009)). As Keser [SOURCE: Page_128]

and Gardner mentioned, if individuals in a CPR game do not play Nash equilibrium, [SOURCE: Page_128]

then policies based on that equilibrium's predictions are suspect. Experimental data [SOURCE: Page_128]

are therefore a useful complement to theory to compare the rules. Section 4 presents [SOURCE: Page_128]

the results of the experiment. We analyze whether the sharing rule has an impact on [SOURCE: Page_128]

self-insurance levels, e ciency and reliability as well as on coordination levels around [SOURCE: Page_128]

the optimal use of the CPR. [SOURCE: Page_128]

3.4 Results The results are presented according to three dimensions. We rst compare the use of [SOURCE: Page_128]

the CPR and the costly substitute in the dierent treatments. Then, we compare the [SOURCE: Page_128]

performance of the rules in terms of e ciency and reliability. As lower performance can [SOURCE: Page_128]

be due to the di culty of subjects to coordinate, we nally compare the rules according [SOURCE: Page_128]

to their performance as coordination devices. [SOURCE: Page_128]

For each indicator, we rst consider some descriptive results on the eect of the rule [SOURCE: Page_128]

using comparisons implied by the treatment plan. We then estimate parametric regres- [SOURCE: Page_128]

sion models to control for more potentially confounding co-variates and to gain further [SOURCE: Page_128]

insights.   Tables 3.4 to 3.7 present the results and are at the end of the chapter. [SOURCE: Page_128]

3.4.1 Coordination on optimal total claim According to theoretical predictions, the total claim to B should be the same in all [SOURCE: Page_128]

treatments (equal to the optimal total claim of 12 units).     Median total claims to B [SOURCE: Page_128]

over all periods are equal to the optimal claim for all treatments (   Table 3.4 ). Figure 3.3 presents the evolution of the median total claim to B in a pair over all periods. The no allocation rule leads to a total claim closer to the optimum than the other rules in [SOURCE: Page_128]

the rst period. After 10 periods, the median total claim converges toward the optimal [SOURCE: Page_128]

solution in treatments CEA as well. The deviation from optimum remains positive for [SOURCE: Page_128]

the CEL and PROP rule in the last period. [SOURCE: Page_128]

127 [SOURCE: Page_128]

Figure 3.3: Median Pair Claim to B [SOURCE: Page_129]

We run nonparametric two-sided Mann-Whitney tests with exactly one summary statis- [SOURCE: Page_129]

tic value per pair (in order to satisfy the statistical independence required for this kind 11 of a test):     the median total claim to B in a pair over all periods.                One observes [SOURCE: Page_129]

that the median total claim is signi cantly lower for the NA rule compared to each of [SOURCE: Page_129]

the bankruptcy rules but there is no signi cant dierence across the bankruptcy rules [SOURCE: Page_129]

(   Table 3.5 ). The hypothesis of equal total claim to B with all the four rules is thus partially rejected and we observe a small treatment eect. However, the simple descrip- [SOURCE: Page_129]

tive statistics may not deliver good evidence as the high variability in decisions make [SOURCE: Page_129]

it hard to detect signi cant dierences in the statistical noise. Therefore, we provide [SOURCE: Page_129]

additional results from multivariate regression models which evaluate the impact of the [SOURCE: Page_129]

treatment on the decisions made by pairs. [SOURCE: Page_129]

11. The use of the median (rather than the mean) is favored in order to reduce the impact of large deviations from the optimal claim observed in very few groups. We also ran the tests on the mean total claim to B and obtained similar results. [SOURCE: Page_129]

128 [SOURCE: Page_129]

Column 1 of      Table 3.6 presents the results from a random eects generalized least squares regression, where the dependent variable is the absolute dierence between [SOURCE: Page_130]

the observed total claim and the optimal total claim. The deviation of total claim to [SOURCE: Page_130]

account B are left-censored at zero when optimum is reached and right-censored at 28 [SOURCE: Page_130]

(40-12=28).     Since ordinary linear regression models are not suited for this problem, [SOURCE: Page_130]

we rely on Tobit regression. We include treatment dummies as explanatory variables, [SOURCE: Page_130]

omitting the dummy corresponding to the NA treatment. We nd that deviation from [SOURCE: Page_130]

optimal claim is signi cantly higher with the PROP and CEL rules, but there is no [SOURCE: Page_130]

signi cant dierence between the CEA and the NA rule. The deviation from optimal [SOURCE: Page_130]

claim reduces over time (as suggested by the negative sign of the period variable) but the [SOURCE: Page_130]

learning eect occurs at a decreasing rate (positive sign of the squared period variable). [SOURCE: Page_130]

The same pattern is observed by comparing the frequency of optimal claim to B. In [SOURCE: Page_130]

the rst period, 4 pairs out of 20 have reached the optimal claim in the NA treatment [SOURCE: Page_130]

(3/20 pairs in CEA, 2/18 pairs in CEL and 1/20 pair in PROP). In the last period, [SOURCE: Page_130]

11 pairs out of 20 have reached the optimal claim in the NA treatment (10/20 pairs in [SOURCE: Page_130]

CEA, 5/18 pairs in CEL and 7/20 pair in PROP). It con rms the catch-up of the CEA [SOURCE: Page_130]

rule whereas the CEL and PROP rules have a higher frequency of coordination failure. [SOURCE: Page_130]

Figure 3.4 presents the % of groups where pairs deviate positively (total claim >12), negatively (total claim <12),and do not deviate (total claim =12), from optimal claim [SOURCE: Page_130]

for the last 10 periods (each group appears 10 times). The CEA rule and the NA rule 12 have the highest frequency of optimal claim and the lowest frequency of shortage. [SOURCE: Page_130]

Shortage frequency is not a su cient indicator of performance as a total claim lower [SOURCE: Page_130]

than low resource size reduces the risk of shortage but it is also suboptimal. The NA [SOURCE: Page_130]

rule has the highest frequency of claims lower than optimal claims (frequency of 30%): [SOURCE: Page_130]

it is suboptimal as it provides too strong incentives to self-insure to the subjects. The [SOURCE: Page_130]

CEL rule creates heterogeneous reactions among subjects: total claim to B is higher [SOURCE: Page_130]

than optimal in 49% of the cases, but it is also lower than optimal in 27% of the cases. [SOURCE: Page_130]

12. When the total claim is higher than the optimal claim, shortage occurs with probability one half (it occurs only in the low scenario). [SOURCE: Page_130]

129 [SOURCE: Page_130]

Figure 3.4: Frequency of Deviation from Optimal Total Claim to B [SOURCE: Page_131]

We found some evidence that the CEA and NA rules lead more often to CPR claims [SOURCE: Page_131]

which are closer to the optimal claim. This can be explained by the fact that the cost [SOURCE: Page_131]

of deviation from equilibrium is higher for the NA rule and this rule therefore provides [SOURCE: Page_131]

more incentives to coordinate on the total optimal claim. On the contrary, the PROP [SOURCE: Page_131]

and CEL rules create incentives to increase the individual claims to B since if the total [SOURCE: Page_131]

claim is higher than resource size, the share of the resource received by each subject [SOURCE: Page_131]

increases with the individual claim to B. Such an incentive is not present for the CEA [SOURCE: Page_131]

rule, which therefore lead to a total claim closer to the optimal claim. However, the [SOURCE: Page_131]

objective for the regulator is not to reach optimal claim to B per se but rather to [SOURCE: Page_131]

maximize total gain from resource use.     We therefore compare total gains generated [SOURCE: Page_131]

under each rule. [SOURCE: Page_131]

3.4.2 E ciency and Reliability E ciency ratio are often used in experiments to compare the observed gains with the [SOURCE: Page_131]

potential maximum gains (in our case, equilibrium gains). Because the total claim to [SOURCE: Page_131]

B is equal for all the rules at equilibrium, the equilibrium total gains are also equal: [SOURCE: Page_131]

34.4 Euros for a pair. As a result, there is no dierence between comparing treatments [SOURCE: Page_131]

according to total gains or e ciency ratio (the denominator is the same). The e ciency [SOURCE: Page_131]

is high with the four instruments (95,5% in average). The median total gains over all [SOURCE: Page_131]

periods are not signi cantly dierent across rules (see non-parametric test in   Table 3.5 ). [SOURCE: Page_131]

130 [SOURCE: Page_131]

However, further results provide some evidence of signi cant dierences across rules. [SOURCE: Page_132]

Column 2 of Table 3.6 presents the results of a random eects generalized least squares regression, where the dependent variable is the total gains in a pair (the average between [SOURCE: Page_132]

total gains if head is drawn and total gains of tail is drawn). The gains are higher with [SOURCE: Page_132]

the CEA rule but not signi cantly. In   column 3 of Table 3.6 , we control for the absolute deviation from optimal claim. Unsurprisingly, the lower the deviation, the higher the [SOURCE: Page_132]

total gains. Even if the observed claims are closer to optimum with the no allocation [SOURCE: Page_132]

rule than with the three bankruptcy rules, average total gains remain lower. This is [SOURCE: Page_132]

due to the fact that no resource is allocated in the low scenario when the total claim [SOURCE: Page_132]

is higher than resource size, whereas there are l units available. On the contrary, the [SOURCE: Page_132]

bankruptcy rules enable to allocate fully the resource available and therefore lead to [SOURCE: Page_132]

higher pro ts. [SOURCE: Page_132]

E ciency alone is not su cient to judge the performance of an instrument. Another [SOURCE: Page_132]

signi cant feature to take into account is the variability of e ciency, which gives an [SOURCE: Page_132]

insight in the reliability of instruments (Cochard et al. (2005)). Indeed, the regulator [SOURCE: Page_132]

in charge of choosing a sharing rule may not only be interested in a rule that performs [SOURCE: Page_132]

well in average but also in a rule that leads to foreseeable and reliable outcomes. The [SOURCE: Page_132]

standard deviation of total gains is taken as a measure of reliability. It is signi cantly [SOURCE: Page_132]

higher with the NA and PROP rule (test of equality of variance in       Table 3.5 ). Most of the variance is due to inter-group variability (as opposed to inter-period variability). [SOURCE: Page_132]

The higher variance with the no allocation rule is due to the fact that no resource is [SOURCE: Page_132]

allocated when the resource size is low if the total claim is higher than the resource [SOURCE: Page_132]

available. Comparatively, the dierence between high and low scenarios is lower when [SOURCE: Page_132]

resource is shared according to the three other sharing rules. [SOURCE: Page_132]

Rules have been compared according to their e ciency and reliability. The CEA rule is [SOURCE: Page_132]

the best rule according to these criteria. The next sub-section presents further insights [SOURCE: Page_132]

on the di culty of subjects to coordinate on the optimal claim to the CPR. Rules are [SOURCE: Page_132]

compared according to their ability to facilitate subjects' coordination. [SOURCE: Page_132]

131 [SOURCE: Page_132]

3.4.3 What explains coordination ? Coordination is analyzed according to three criteria.    Firstly, under the presence of [SOURCE: Page_133]

multiple theoretical equilibria, we look at which equilibrium more often arises.    The [SOURCE: Page_133]

dierences in coordination success across rules can be due to the number of equilibria. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Secondly, beliefs are analyzed and prediction errors are compared across rules.     The [SOURCE: Page_133]

ability to predict what the other is going to play can potentially be impacted by the [SOURCE: Page_133]

complexity of the sharing rule.   Lastly, we compare best-response rates.     Failure to [SOURCE: Page_133]

best-respond is another potential determinant of dierences in coordination level across [SOURCE: Page_133]

rules. It may be easier for subjects to nd out what is the aggregate equilibrium and [SOURCE: Page_133]

the best response under some rules than others. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Coordination under the presence of multiple equilibria [SOURCE: Page_133]

The total Nash equilibrium claim is equal for all rules but there are multiple individual [SOURCE: Page_133]

Nash equilibria as presented in   Table 3.3.    We look at individual data in order to [SOURCE: Page_133]

determine which equilibria arise more frequently.    Figure 3.5 presents the frequency of choice of each possible individual claim to B for each rule in the last period (it is [SOURCE: Page_133]

assumed that the coordination is maximal in the last period). Claiming 6 units to B [SOURCE: Page_133]

is clearly a focal point in all treatments. It is the median as well as the modal choice, [SOURCE: Page_133]

with 42.5% of the subjects making that choice for the NA rule, 55% for the CEA rule, [SOURCE: Page_133]

22% for the PROP rule and 25% for the CEL rule. [SOURCE: Page_133]

132 [SOURCE: Page_133]

Figure 3.5: Frequency of individual claim to B (last period) [SOURCE: Page_134]

In the rst period, 8 of the 10 pairs that have converged towards the total optimal [SOURCE: Page_134]

claim across all treatments coordinate on the symmetric equilibrium with each subject [SOURCE: Page_134]

claiming 6 units. In the last period (period 10 or 20 according to the sessions), 24 of [SOURCE: Page_134]

the 33 pairs that have converged towards the total optimal claim coordinate on the [SOURCE: Page_134]

symmetric equilibrium with each subject claiming 6 units. Other equilibria observed [SOURCE: Page_134]

in the last period are (b^1 ; b^2 ) = (0; 12) (3 pairs), [(1; 11)] (2 pairs), (2; 10) (2 pairs) and [SOURCE: Page_134]

(5; 7) (3 pairs). There is signi cant dierences across rules in the variance of individual choices. Graph- [SOURCE: Page_134]

ically (   Figure 3.5 ), we observe more heterogeneous choices with CEL and PROP rules as compared to the CEA and NA rules. The distribution of individual claims in the last [SOURCE: Page_134]

period has a sharper peak and shorter thinner tails in the CEA and NA treatments, [SOURCE: Page_134]

while the distribution has more rounded peak and fatter tails in the PROP and CEL [SOURCE: Page_134]

rules. We observe individual claims to B above 12 only for the PROP and the CEL rule [SOURCE: Page_134]

in the last period. These subjects bet on the draw of a tail but if head is drawn, [SOURCE: Page_134]

they are sure to receive less units than their claim.       More risky choices are observed [SOURCE: Page_134]

with the CEL and PROP rules as they create greater incentives to increase claims of [SOURCE: Page_134]

free units (from the B account) since the number of units received from B depends [SOURCE: Page_134]

133 [SOURCE: Page_134]

positively on claims to this account. All these results suggest that coordination is more [SOURCE: Page_135]

di cult under the CEL treatment. [SOURCE: Page_135]

In order to understand the determinant of individual decisions, we report in               Table 3.7 additional results from multivariate regression models which evaluate the contribution [SOURCE: Page_135]

of dierent factors on the decisions made by each subject individually. We run random [SOURCE: Page_135]

eects generalized least squares Tobit regressions, where the dependent variable is the 13 individual claim to account B (censored between 0 and 20).                They con rm that claims [SOURCE: Page_135]

are signi cantly higher with the bankruptcy rules as compared to the NA rule. The [SOURCE: Page_135]

coe cient of the PROP rule is the highest as this rule creates incentives to claim more [SOURCE: Page_135]

to B. Risk aversion signi cantly reduces individual claims to B at the 1% level, whereas [SOURCE: Page_135]

aversion to strategic uncertainty has also a negative but less signi cant eect (10% [SOURCE: Page_135]

level). [SOURCE: Page_135]

The subjects are expected to adjust their individual claims according to the total claim [SOURCE: Page_135]

to B as they learn this information after each period .               Of course this can be the [SOURCE: Page_135]

case only if they guess what is the total optimal claim. In columns 2 and 3 of                 Table 3.7, the regression includes a dummy variable indicating if total claim to B in the previous period was higher (positive deviation) or lower (negative deviation) than the [SOURCE: Page_135]

optimal claim (the reference situation is when the total claim is optimal in the previous [SOURCE: Page_135]

period).   We run two dierent regressions for the NA and the bankruptcy rules as [SOURCE: Page_135]

the results strongly dier.      In the NA treatment, as expected, subjects signi cantly [SOURCE: Page_135]

reduce their claims if the total claim was higher than 12 in the previous previous. As [SOURCE: Page_135]

already mentioned, the incentives to do so are higher in the NA treatment than in the [SOURCE: Page_135]

bankruptcy treatments because the cost of deviation is higher.                On the contrary, in [SOURCE: Page_135]

the bankruptcy rules regression, we nd that subjects signi cantly increase their claims [SOURCE: Page_135]

when total claim deviates from 12, whatever the sign of the deviation (compare to the [SOURCE: Page_135]

case where there is no deviation).           Its surprising to note that subjects increase their [SOURCE: Page_135]

claims when the total claim is already too high. This results are mainly driven by the [SOURCE: Page_135]

PROP rule: if subjects realize that coordination will fail anyway, they are better o [SOURCE: Page_135]

increasing their own claim in order to get an higher share of the resource. [SOURCE: Page_135]

13. We also run a complementary random eects generalized least squares regressions, where we cluster the errors at the pair level to capture any unobserved heterogeneity in the pair (the tobit model does not enable to cluster error terms). As the results are similar in both regressions, we only presents the results from the Tobit model. [SOURCE: Page_135]

134 [SOURCE: Page_135]

Prediction errors and failure to best-respond [SOURCE: Page_136]

We further study two possible explanations to coordination failure: di culty in antici- [SOURCE: Page_136]

pating others' behavior and failure to best respond to expectations. We investigate the [SOURCE: Page_136]

impact of these two biases in the dierent treatments. [SOURCE: Page_136]

The more accurately the outcome of a game can be predicted the lower the strategic [SOURCE: Page_136]

uncertainty and the more eective the coordination and ultimately the design of mech- [SOURCE: Page_136]

anisms used to establish e cient outcomes (Heinemann et al. (2009)).        We measure [SOURCE: Page_136]

predictability by the prediction error - equal to the dierence between stated beliefs on [SOURCE: Page_136]

the other's claim to B and the observed claim of the other -. Average errors are de- [SOURCE: Page_136]

creasing over time ( Figure 3.6 ). Column 1 of Table 3.8 presents results from a random eect linear regression model for the error of prediction. The errors are higher in all [SOURCE: Page_136]

treatments compared to the no allocation rule but the treatment eect is signi cant [SOURCE: Page_136]

only for the CEL rule. Errors decrease over time but learning occurs at a decreasing [SOURCE: Page_136]

rate (as suggested by signs of the period and sq-period variables). This learning eect [SOURCE: Page_136]

is observable on   Figure 3.6. We have no particular explanation for the observed sharp increase in the average prediction error in periods 16 and 17 with the CEL rule. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Figure 3.6: Average Error of Prediction [SOURCE: Page_136]

135 [SOURCE: Page_136]

In order to achieve coordination, subjects must not only be able to predict accurately [SOURCE: Page_137]

the other player's behavior but also to make their own choice such as too reach the [SOURCE: Page_137]

best aggregate outcome. In this game, subjects should coordinate on claiming a total [SOURCE: Page_137]

of 12 units.   We compare stated beliefs and decisions and measure the best-response [SOURCE: Page_137]

rate. A best response is such that the claim to B is equal to the dierence in absolute [SOURCE: Page_137]

value between the total optimal claim 12 and the belief on the other's claim to B. The [SOURCE: Page_137]

best response rate is the percentage of subjects that best respond to their beliefs on [SOURCE: Page_137]

the other's claim. [SOURCE: Page_137]

Figure 3.7 shows the evolution of best-response rate over time.     The best-response [SOURCE: Page_137]

rate are signi cantly dierent across treatments. The best-response rate is low for the [SOURCE: Page_137]

CEA rule at the beginning but learning is higher for this rule.   CEA has the higher [SOURCE: Page_137]

best-response rate in the last period.   However, according to a chi-square frequency [SOURCE: Page_137]

test, these rates are not signi cantly dierent from a random distribution (probability [SOURCE: Page_137]

of 50% of chance to best respond) in the last period. Column 2 of   Table 3.8 presents results from a random eect Probit model explaining the probability of best responding. [SOURCE: Page_137]

The Probit model examines the factor the subjects' decisions to best respond or not. [SOURCE: Page_137]

The estimated probability to best respond is signi cantly lower in all the bankruptcy [SOURCE: Page_137]

treatments as compared to the reference (NA): the probability is reduced by 38% for [SOURCE: Page_137]

CEL, 30% for CEA and 27% for PROP (marginal eects). The lower cost of deviation [SOURCE: Page_137]

with the bankruptcy rules can explain that subjects best respond less often. Individual [SOURCE: Page_137]

risk preferences have no signi cant eect on the probability to best respond. Subjects [SOURCE: Page_137]

learn over time (at decreasing rate) as the probability to best respond increases with [SOURCE: Page_137]

period.   Column 3 of   Table 3.8 presents estimates from a random eects regression model on the magnitude of deviation from the best-response claim. The results mirror [SOURCE: Page_137]

those from the Probit model and show that the amount of deviation (in absolute value) [SOURCE: Page_137]

is signi cantly higher in the three bankruptcy treatments compared to the no allocation [SOURCE: Page_137]

rule and decreases over time. [SOURCE: Page_137]

136 [SOURCE: Page_137]

Figure 3.7: Average Best-Response Rate [SOURCE: Page_138]

Finally, we compare deviation from coordination - de ned as the dierence between [SOURCE: Page_138]

the observed claim and the claim that would guarantee that equilibrium is reached, [SOURCE: Page_138]

which is equal to 12 minus the claim of the other -. Both deviation from best response [SOURCE: Page_138]

and prediction error contribute to coordination failure. Notice that the deviation from [SOURCE: Page_138]

coordination is not necessarily equal to the sum of deviation from best response and [SOURCE: Page_138]

prediction error as the two eects may cancel out.     Column 4 of   Table 3.8 presents estimates from a random eects linear regression model on the magnitude of deviation [SOURCE: Page_138]

from coordination. The model speci cation includes the amount of deviation from the [SOURCE: Page_138]

best-response claim and the prediction error, on top of the usual treatments and risk [SOURCE: Page_138]

preferences variables.   Both the failure to best respond and the error in prediction [SOURCE: Page_138]

explain signi cantly the amount of deviation from coordination, with the rst factor [SOURCE: Page_138]

having a stronger eect. It suggests that the most di cult task is not to predict the [SOURCE: Page_138]

other's decision but to understand what is the aggregate equilibrium.      There is no [SOURCE: Page_138]

remaining treatment eect once the deviation from best response and the prediction [SOURCE: Page_138]

error are taken into account. [SOURCE: Page_138]

The results are summarized in   Table 3.3, where the four rules are ranked from 1 (best) to 4 (worse) according to each criterion.   Taking into account all criteria, the most [SOURCE: Page_138]

performant rule is the CEA rule. [SOURCE: Page_138]

3.5 Conclusion When CPR users have ex-ante claims over the resource and resource size is not su cient [SOURCE: Page_140]

to cover all the individual claims, there is a need to establish a scarcity-sharing rule. [SOURCE: Page_140]

When agents have access to a substitute secure resource, rules should not only allocate [SOURCE: Page_140]

the resource in case of scarcity but also facilitate the coordination of individual deci- [SOURCE: Page_140]

sions on the optimal resource use, i.e. the optimal claim to the CPR and the optimal [SOURCE: Page_140]

investment in a secure resource. There is a trade-o between having access to a free [SOURCE: Page_140]

resource and supporting the risk of shortage. Investing a total of l = 12 units in the [SOURCE: Page_140]

safe resource maximizes both individual and total gains. The sharing rule can act as a [SOURCE: Page_140]

coordination mechanism to in uence the coordination towards a total claim to the CPR [SOURCE: Page_140]

equal to the optimal claim.   We compare rules that are described in the bankruptcy [SOURCE: Page_140]

literature to share the available resource and used in the practice of water management: [SOURCE: Page_140]

the constrained-equal awards, the constrained-equal losses and the proportional rules. [SOURCE: Page_140]

The no allocation rule (the traditional rule used in CPR game) is used as a benchmark. [SOURCE: Page_140]

In theory, the four rules lead to the same total claim to the CPR and the same e ciency [SOURCE: Page_140]

at equilibrium. However, we observe dierences across rules in the experiment. [SOURCE: Page_140]

We nd that the most e cient coordination institution is the no allocation rule but [SOURCE: Page_140]

e ciency and reliability are higher with the constrained-equal awards rule.        The no [SOURCE: Page_140]

allocation rule favors coordination as it is easy to understand and the costs of coor- [SOURCE: Page_140]

dination failure are high.   However, the total gains are reduced and the variability [SOURCE: Page_140]

increased as no resource is allocated in case of over-lapping claims. The rule facilitates [SOURCE: Page_140]

coordination but at the cost of reduced e ciency.      We nd that the best rule is the [SOURCE: Page_140]

constrained-awards rules as its simplicity facilitates coordination and it limits strategic [SOURCE: Page_140]

interactions between agents. Rules which are de ned as a proportion of claims such as [SOURCE: Page_140]

the PROP and CEL rules induce sub-optimal investments in alternative safe resources [SOURCE: Page_140]

and therefore lead to an increase in the frequency and severity of shortage. [SOURCE: Page_140]

Our work can contribute to the improvement of scarcity sharing rule implemented in [SOURCE: Page_140]

the elds. France is presently implementing a water reform in order to adjust to the [SOURCE: Page_140]

increased frequency of drought. A rst message to policy makers is that water restriction [SOURCE: Page_140]

rules which are de ned as a proportion of existing pumping rights or licences create [SOURCE: Page_140]

strategic interactions among farmers and are likely to induce sub-optimal investments [SOURCE: Page_140]

in alternative safe resources (such as reservoirs). This is an important result since many [SOURCE: Page_140]

countries do rely on such restriction rules. For example in Australia, water rights held [SOURCE: Page_140]

139 [SOURCE: Page_140]

by farmers determine their water allocation as a proportion of total water available for [SOURCE: Page_141]

agriculture. In some regions in Spain, volumes distributed in times of shortage are also [SOURCE: Page_141]

calculated as proportions of the subscribed quotas. According to our results, such rules [SOURCE: Page_141]

can potentially lead to too low levels of self-insurance, lower pro ts from irrigation and [SOURCE: Page_141]

higher variability. Our result suggests that the French practice of sharing water through [SOURCE: Page_141]

temporary restriction roster systems (where left bank farmers are allowed to irrigate on [SOURCE: Page_141]

the rst two days of the week, and right bank farmers are allocated the last two days) [SOURCE: Page_141]

is not ine cient as it is close to the constrained-equal awards rule. The restrictions are [SOURCE: Page_141]

independent from volumetric licences and this reduces the incentives of farmers to rely [SOURCE: Page_141]

too much on the river water. [SOURCE: Page_141]

The work should be extended to capture further elements of the eld reality and there- [SOURCE: Page_141]

fore increase the external validity of the results. For example, the heterogeneity that [SOURCE: Page_141]

exists in the elds among farmers is not captured in the experiment.        Further work [SOURCE: Page_141]

could include an extension to bigger groups with subjects of dierent types within the [SOURCE: Page_141]

group to see what is the more performant rule in an heterogeneous environment. Our [SOURCE: Page_141]

theoretical work presented in chapter 2 suggests that the optimal sharing rule in the [SOURCE: Page_141]

presence of heterogeneous agents depend on the marginal value from resource use and [SOURCE: Page_141]

risk tolerance. We could also run a treatment where the rule is unknown at the mo- [SOURCE: Page_141]

ment of deciding the claims to each resource. The available resource would be shared [SOURCE: Page_141]

randomly once its size is known. The use of this alternative benchmark could provide [SOURCE: Page_141]

insights on the gains from announcing clearly the sharing rule and see whether these [SOURCE: Page_141]

gains dier according to the rule.   Contrarily to the rst intuition, one may observe [SOURCE: Page_141]

that the announcement of the rule reduces the incentives of the agents to self-insure [SOURCE: Page_141]

and therefore reduces e ciency. An other treatment would introduce the possibility for [SOURCE: Page_141]

subjects to simultaneously choose the sharing rule (with a collective voting procedure [SOURCE: Page_141]

as in Gachter and Riedl (2006), Herrero et al. (2010)) and their claims to the secure [SOURCE: Page_141]

resource and the CPR. Such a treatment will better capture the essence of the ongoing [SOURCE: Page_141]

reform in France: committees will be constituted at the sub-basin level (Organismes [SOURCE: Page_141]

Uniques) and responsible for collectively de ning the sharing rule simultaneously with [SOURCE: Page_141]

the allocation of water volumetric licences. Finally, conducting a eld experiment with [SOURCE: Page_141]

farmers (with non-neutral terms) could increase the external validity comparing with [SOURCE: Page_141]

a laboratory experiment with students. If farmers may have a better understanding of [SOURCE: Page_141]

the issues at stake around water, it's not obvious how it will impact the coordination [SOURCE: Page_141]

in the experiment. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Appendix 3.A: Nash equilibrium under risk aversion We extend the equilibrium results presented in section 3.3 to the case where agents are 0 not risk-neutral (with Ui a well-behaved increasing utility function with Ui                          > 0 and Ui^00 < 0)). The objective function becomes: [SOURCE: Page_147]

Appendix 3.B: Experimental instructions The instructions reported are translated from French by a native French speaker who is [SOURCE: Page_149]

uent in English. The text in italics has been added to make the instructions clearer for the reader, the participants did not see this . The experiment compounds of 3 parts. In parts 1 and 2, you'll make a unique decision. Part 3 is compounds of several periods (     the exact number is not annonced ) and you'll make one decision and one prediction in each period. [SOURCE: Page_149]

Earnings You'll be paid in Cash at the end of the experiment for one of your decision or prediction. This will be randomly selected by the central computer at the end of the experiment. Prior to receiving your payment, you will realize a coin ip at the experimenter desk when a random draw is necessary to calculate your nal earnings. [SOURCE: Page_149]

Decision A decision consists in claiming a total of 20 units in two accounts (A and B). You will receive units that will give you 1 Euro each. The number of units received can be lower, equal or higher than the number of units claimed. It depends on the characteristics of each account that will be dierent in each part of the experiment. [SOURCE: Page_149]

You will indicate to the computer the number of units you want to claim in each account (an integer). There are 21 possibilities (A ;B) where A is the number of units claimed in account A and B the number of units claimed in account B : (20 ;0)(19 ;1)(18 ;2)(17 ;3)(16 ;4)(15 ;5)(14 ;6) (13 ;7)(12 ;8)(11 ;9)(10 ;10)(9 ;11)(8 ;12)(7 ;13)(6 ;14)(5 ;15)(4 ;16)(3 ;17)(2 ;18)(1 ;19)(0 ;20). The computer will verify that the total is equal to 20 units when you are entering your two claims. [SOURCE: Page_149]

Next page presents the instuctions of part 1. Instructions of part 2 and 3 will be distri- buted once part 1 has ended. [SOURCE: Page_149]

There is no cost to claim units in account B. [SOURCE: Page_150]

The number of unit received from account B depends on a coin ip. [SOURCE: Page_150]

• If tail is drawn, you will receive 2 units for each unit claimed in account B • If head is drawn, you will receive 0 units from account B You don't know the outcome of the coin ip when you are taking your decision. You will only ip the coin at the end of the experiment if this decision is selected for payment. [SOURCE: Page_150]

## ACCOUNT B :

Account B is a joint account for you and the other member of your pair. [SOURCE: Page_152]

There is no cost to claim units in account B. [SOURCE: Page_152]

The number of unit received from account B depends on the total claim to account B. The total claim is equal to your claim plus the claim of the other member of your pair. [SOURCE: Page_152]

• If the total claim to B is lower or equal to 20 units, you will receive 2 units for each unit claimed in account B [SOURCE: Page_152]

• If the total claim to B is strictly higher than 20 units, you will receive 0 units from account B [SOURCE: Page_152]

You don't know the decision of the other when you are taking your decision as you take your decisions simultaneously. [SOURCE: Page_152]

Table 3.10 presents the earnings in Euros for each possible choice according to the total claim to B. Your decisions are indexed by 1 (A$_1$ ; B$_1$ ) and the decisions of the other by 2 (A$_2$ ; B$_2$ ). Earnings are computed as follows : [SOURCE: Page_152]

If B$_1$ + B$_2$ ≤ 20 : (1 × A$_1$ + 2 × B$_1$ ) × 1 − 0, 2 × A$_1$ Euros. If B$_1$ + B$_2$ > 20 : (1 × A$_1$ + 0 × B$_1$ ) × 1 − 0, 2 × A$_1$ Euros [SOURCE: Page_152]

Part 3 For each of the periods of part 3, you'll have to make one decision and one prediction. Your decision consists in claiming a total of 20 units in two accounts (A and B). You will also have to indicate your prediction concerning the number of units the other member of your pair is going to claim to account B. [SOURCE: Page_154]

The characteristics of account A and B are identical in all periods. [SOURCE: Page_154]
