5.1 Introduction Water markets are acknowledged to allocate scarce water e ciently by encouraging wa- [SOURCE: Page_185]

ter conservation and by moving water from low to high value uses. Although there is [SOURCE: Page_185]

now widespread adoption of trading mechanisms to re-allocate water, there is an ongo- [SOURCE: Page_185]

ing debate on how to improve the trading process. The existing literature has mainly [SOURCE: Page_185]

focused on two aspects of water trading:           trading constraints and transactions costs [SOURCE: Page_185]

which reduce e ciency gains (Gardner and Fullerton (1968), Colby (1990), Allen Con- [SOURCE: Page_185]

sulting Group (2006), Carey et al. (2002)); and third party impacts when trade might [SOURCE: Page_185]

aect other users who are not involved in the transaction (Productivity Commission [SOURCE: Page_185]

(2006, 2010), Bourgeon et al. (2008)). However, another important issue that deserves [SOURCE: Page_185]

attention is the role that water markets can play to mitigate the risk of water shortage 1 for farmers. [SOURCE: Page_185]

The co-existence of both a market for water rights and a market for water allocation [SOURCE: Page_185]

has been recognized as a useful risk management tool for irrigating farmers (Bjornlund [SOURCE: Page_185]

(2006)). In most countries where water can be traded, farmers can buy or sell water [SOURCE: Page_185]

rights, which entitle them to a given share of total available water within a season. [SOURCE: Page_185]

The water volume that each right holder gets is called his allocation. When needed, [SOURCE: Page_185]

farmers can also buy or sell water on the seasonal allocation market. Water markets [SOURCE: Page_185]

improve the e ciency of water allocation and reduce farmers' exposure to risk as they [SOURCE: Page_185]

transform the risk related to the availability of the water input into a risk on input [SOURCE: Page_185]

prices (Calatrava and Garrido (2005)). Moreover, farmers are better able to manage [SOURCE: Page_185]

their risks by shifting from the right market to the allocation market (and vice-versa) [SOURCE: Page_185]

according to production conditions. [SOURCE: Page_185]

Under increasing pressure to manage water more e ciently, water trade institutions are [SOURCE: Page_185]

being progressively strengthened, as the bene ts of doing so (in terms of property rights [SOURCE: Page_185]

being well-de ned and markets becoming more complete) can oset the inherent costs [SOURCE: Page_185]

involved (Bjornlund and Rossini (2008), Libecap (2011)). Under most statutory water [SOURCE: Page_185]

right laws, as in Chile, Mexico and most of Australia, water rights are all identical in [SOURCE: Page_185]

terms of security. They are de ned as a proportion of stream ow or storage capacity [SOURCE: Page_185]

and the total available water in a season is shared between water right holders in [SOURCE: Page_185]

proportion to the volumetric rights they own. In other countries and states however, [SOURCE: Page_185]

1. While this problem is particularly crucial for unregulated river systems where there is no water storage through reservoir dams, it also exists for regulated systems because the probability of reserve replenishment from one year to another is seen to increasingly uctuate with climatic change. [SOURCE: Page_185]

184 [SOURCE: Page_185]

water rights can have dierent security levels, with the holders of high security rights [SOURCE: Page_186]

being served rst in case of scarcity. Once provisions for high security water rights have [SOURCE: Page_186]

been made, the remaining volume of water determines the low security allocations. As [SOURCE: Page_186]

a result, low security water right holders bear the bulk of the risk of low water supply. [SOURCE: Page_186]

Such hierarchy of rights is also the basis of the prior appropriation doctrine, much in [SOURCE: Page_186]

use in western United States: when shortage occurs, priority is given to the most senior [SOURCE: Page_186]

rights, those which were historically appropriated rst.     When a water right is sold, [SOURCE: Page_186]

it retains its original appropriation date.   The security of a water-right can thus be [SOURCE: Page_186]

purchased by buying a senior right on the right market. Since 1994, some Australian [SOURCE: Page_186]

states (New South Wales and Victoria) have created a dierentiated water right system. [SOURCE: Page_186]

Farmers can constitute a portfolio of water rights of two security levels. It is expected [SOURCE: Page_186]

that a dierentiated water rights system would improve the e ciency of resource and [SOURCE: Page_186]

risk allocation across farmers.   However such reforms are administratively complex [SOURCE: Page_186]

and can lead to substantial transaction and learning costs for water users.         Before [SOURCE: Page_186]

encouraging a wider adoption of such complex systems, it is necessary to understand [SOURCE: Page_186]

and evaluate whether they can lead to genuine gains for water users. This question is [SOURCE: Page_186]

at the heart of a number of recent water market reforms. [SOURCE: Page_186]

The objective of this article is to compare the allocative e ciency, cost-eectiveness and [SOURCE: Page_186]

risk management properties of a market with two security levels for water rights relative [SOURCE: Page_186]

to a market with a unique type of water right. There is insu cient eld data to examine [SOURCE: Page_186]

this research question using statistical techniques because water rights markets are still [SOURCE: Page_186]

rather thin. For example, in Australia, little activity is observed yet in the market for [SOURCE: Page_186]

low security rights (National Water Comission (2009)) and in the western United States, [SOURCE: Page_186]

the trading of senior water rights is restricted by regulations controlling third party ef- [SOURCE: Page_186]

fects (Libecap (2011)).   As a result, there are too few situations where farmers can [SOURCE: Page_186]

freely constitute a portfolio of water rights with dierent levels of security. Moreover, [SOURCE: Page_186]

dierences between countries or states in terms of hydrology and socio-economic envi- [SOURCE: Page_186]

ronment can make the comparison of the performance across the systems di cult. This [SOURCE: Page_186]

article therefore uses data from laboratory experiments.     It presents an experimental [SOURCE: Page_186]

design that captures the main characteristics of existing water markets. [SOURCE: Page_186]

The design is noteworthy in two respects. First, we introduce two markets in our ex- [SOURCE: Page_186]

periment: a market for water rights and a market for water allocation.        While other [SOURCE: Page_186]

experimental studies have explored one market in isolation (for example, Cristi and [SOURCE: Page_186]

Alevy (2009) and Garrido (2007b) focus on the allocation market, whereas Hansen [SOURCE: Page_186]

185 [SOURCE: Page_186]

et al. (2007) include the allocation market and an option market but no rights market), [SOURCE: Page_187]

this article is the rst to consider both markets. In the emission trading experimental [SOURCE: Page_187]

literature, Godby et al. (1997) designed an experiment mimicking the Canadian emis- [SOURCE: Page_187]

sions trading market including both a share and a coupon market.                    A coupon gives [SOURCE: Page_187]

permission to discharge a unit quantity of waste.            A share represents an entitlement [SOURCE: Page_187]

to a speci ed fraction of the total available coupons to be issued in future periods. [SOURCE: Page_187]

Our experiment is inspired by this design. Subjects rst participate in a share market [SOURCE: Page_187]

(corresponding to the water rights market) without knowing the allocation of coupons [SOURCE: Page_187]

(corresponding to water allocation) they will get from their shares. In a second stage, [SOURCE: Page_187]

they can trade their coupons on the coupon market (corresponding to the market for [SOURCE: Page_187]

water allocation). [SOURCE: Page_187]

The second novelty of our design is the introduction of dierent levels of security for [SOURCE: Page_187]

shares which enables us to compare the proportional rights system with the priority [SOURCE: Page_187]

system. While Calatrava and Garrido (2006a) compared these two de nitions of water [SOURCE: Page_187]

rights using simulation data, they do not consider water rights trading in their analy- [SOURCE: Page_187]

sis. To our knowledge this design feature has not been examined systematically using 2 experimental methods. [SOURCE: Page_187]

The two main treatment variables of our experiment are the number of security lev- [SOURCE: Page_187]

els for shares (1 or 2) and the presence of transactions costs in the share and coupon [SOURCE: Page_187]

markets. With these treatments, we examine the role of transactions costs which are [SOURCE: Page_187]

recognized as an important feature of water markets and show how these costs can [SOURCE: Page_187]

impact the performance of a two security levels system. While several researchers have [SOURCE: Page_187]

studied transactions costs in environmental markets (Kerr and Mare (1995), Gangadha- [SOURCE: Page_187]

ran (2000), Cason and Gangadharan (2003)), the impacts of such costs on participants' [SOURCE: Page_187]

decisions to trade in one market relative to the other have been largely ignored. We [SOURCE: Page_187]

nd that while risk allocation improves with a two security levels system irrespective of [SOURCE: Page_187]

which market displays higher transactions costs, the e ciency of water allocation and [SOURCE: Page_187]

the total pro ts generated are more dependent on the con guration of the transactions [SOURCE: Page_187]

costs. [SOURCE: Page_187]

2. Noussair and Porter (1992) ran an auction experiment, inspired by the priority service literature, on proportional versus priority rationing systems (Wilson and Chao (1987), Wilson (1989)). As there is no reconciliation market in their design, the only way to achieve e cient allocation is through the auction and the rationing scheme. Our article on the contrary has a reconciliation mechanism: the coupon market is a kind of reconciliation market in case the allocation is not e cient after the share market. Therefore, e cient allocation is the result of both the coupon and the share market. [SOURCE: Page_187]

186 [SOURCE: Page_187]

This article is organized as follows.        Section 5.2 summarizes the existing literature [SOURCE: Page_188]

on the expected bene ts and limitations of having dierentiated water rights.                   The [SOURCE: Page_188]

experimental design and corresponding theoretical predictions are presented in Sections [SOURCE: Page_188]

5.3 and 5.4. Section 5.5 reports the experimental results and Section 5.6 concludes with [SOURCE: Page_188]

some implications for policy. [SOURCE: Page_188]

5.2 Rationale for dierentiated water rights The bene ts from a water market with dierentiated rights rely on the existence and [SOURCE: Page_188]

e ciency of the water rights market itself. Most of the countries that are engaging in [SOURCE: Page_188]

water market reforms are investing into the creation or the enhancement of water rights [SOURCE: Page_188]

markets, for example through the formal separation of water rights from land rights, [SOURCE: Page_188]

mainly to facilitate real structural change within the irrigation industry. If the water [SOURCE: Page_188]

market is perfectly competitive and transaction-cost free, and if water users are risk- [SOURCE: Page_188]

neutral, then it is well-known that trading on the seasonal allocation market is su cient [SOURCE: Page_188]

to reach an e cient allocation of water amongst users (Freebairn and Quiggin (2006)). [SOURCE: Page_188]

In theory, trading on the water rights market should not occur since all water users have [SOURCE: Page_188]

the same expected value for water rights (expected value of the corresponding allocation [SOURCE: Page_188]

on the allocation market which is the same for all since there is a unique expected price [SOURCE: Page_188]

for water) and thus display the same willingness to pay for rights. Nevertheless, it is [SOURCE: Page_188]

observed that farmers are showing growing interest in water rights trading and water [SOURCE: Page_188]

rights markets are slowly picking up (Young (2010)).             Demand and supply of water [SOURCE: Page_188]

rights are driven by heterogeneous risk attitudes and anticipations (Cristi (2007)), long [SOURCE: Page_188]

term speculation (related to the uncertainty about the level of future water supply) or [SOURCE: Page_188]

saving motives. Some irrigators view high security water rights as a hedge against future [SOURCE: Page_188]

uncertainties and as high value capital assets which can be used as a mortgage guarantee [SOURCE: Page_188]

(Bjornlund (2003), Grafton and Peterson (2007)). A system with dierentiated water [SOURCE: Page_188]

rights allows users to hold a sophisticated portfolio of rights with dierent levels of [SOURCE: Page_188]

security, therefore potentially improving their management of water and the associated 3 risks.       The arguments can be summarized as follows: [SOURCE: Page_188]

Firstly, Freebairn and Quiggin (2006) argue that multiple security levels for water rights [SOURCE: Page_188]

3. In this paper, we focus exclusively on the role that such a system could play in the management of short-term water shortage risks, leaving aside the other bene ts listed above which concern mainly the management of long-term uncertainties. [SOURCE: Page_188]

187 [SOURCE: Page_188]

can improve the cost-eectiveness of water allocation by allowing users to hold rights [SOURCE: Page_189]

which match their water needs in each climatic scenario better, thus reducing trade [SOURCE: Page_189]

on the allocation market and the corresponding transactions costs.         Indeed, despite [SOURCE: Page_189]

the existence of trade-facilitating solutions such as electronic market places or brokers, [SOURCE: Page_189]

trading water remains costly. Transactions costs are incurred in searching for a trading [SOURCE: Page_189]

partner, ascertaining the characteristics of the water commodity, negotiating a price [SOURCE: Page_189]

and other terms of transfer and obtaining legal approval of the transfer (Colby (1990), [SOURCE: Page_189]

Carey et al. (2002), Bjornlund (2003), Allen Consulting Group (2006)). Freebairn and [SOURCE: Page_189]

Quiggin's argument is nevertheless controversial because it relies on the assumption [SOURCE: Page_189]

that transactions costs in the allocation market are greater than transactions costs in [SOURCE: Page_189]

the water rights market. Instead, most water markets seem to display greater transac- [SOURCE: Page_189]

tions costs on the latter, rst because water right transactions are more heavily taxed [SOURCE: Page_189]

than water trading, and second because it is more administratively and legally complex. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Bjornlund (2003) and Brennan (2006) examine this issue for Australian water markets. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Libecap (2011) also mentions the reluctance to trade senior rights in the western US be- [SOURCE: Page_189]

cause of the increasing number of protests and litigation procedures launched by junior [SOURCE: Page_189]

rights holders. As a result, if transactions costs in the rights market are prohibitive, [SOURCE: Page_189]

they might oset the bene ts of active trading in the water rights market, which is [SOURCE: Page_189]

required to constitute a portfolio of rights matching water needs. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Secondly, a dierentiated system can improve both the risk management opportunities [SOURCE: Page_189]

for risk averse farmers, as well as overall risk allocation. Even though water markets [SOURCE: Page_189]

help in reducing the risk born by farmers by converting a quantity risk into a price [SOURCE: Page_189]

risk (Calatrava and Garrido (2005)), they fail to share the remaining risk e ciently [SOURCE: Page_189]

(Howitt (1998)). As underlined by Quiggin (2008),  the quest to eliminate uncertainty [SOURCE: Page_189]

is futile but uncertainty can be managed, allocated and sometimes mitigated .         The [SOURCE: Page_189]

principle of risk allocation (or risk sharing) is that risk should be allocated to the party [SOURCE: Page_189]

best able to manage or accept it.      In principle, this can be achieved through risk- [SOURCE: Page_189]

sharing contracts such as options on the water market or conditional leases of water. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Risk-averse users can trade-o lower expected gains for lower variability of gains and [SOURCE: Page_189]

more risk-tolerant users may be willing to support a greater share of water variability in [SOURCE: Page_189]

exchange of lower prices or higher water volumes in wet seasons. Bjornlund and Rossini [SOURCE: Page_189]

(2008) have studied Australian water markets at length and they suggest that the risk [SOURCE: Page_189]

dierential between high value water users (eg. perennial crops) and producers of annual [SOURCE: Page_189]

crops is su ciently large to enable sophisticated risk-sharing instruments to operate. [SOURCE: Page_189]

188 [SOURCE: Page_189]

Water rights with dierent levels of security can mimic these risk-sharing contracts and [SOURCE: Page_190]

may be easier to implement. It has been observed that uncertainty relating to water [SOURCE: Page_190]

allocation motivates farmers to hold more rights than necessary (Brennan (2006)). With [SOURCE: Page_190]

dierentiated rights, they can instead buy more secure rights. Resource security being [SOURCE: Page_190]

a zero-sum commodity, the more security is given to a group of users, the less there [SOURCE: Page_190]

is for everybody else (Quiggin (2008)). Some users will therefore bear more risks but [SOURCE: Page_190]

they will also bene t from lower water prices. [SOURCE: Page_190]

A two security levels system thus displays two major advantages, compared to a sin- [SOURCE: Page_190]

gle security system: transactions costs saving and improved risk allocation.      On the [SOURCE: Page_190]

negative side however, it increases the complexity of water market management for [SOURCE: Page_190]

the administrators and the complexity of water market participation for the farmers [SOURCE: Page_190]

(Hughes and Goesch (2009), Shi (2006)). Overall, the bene ts of water rights dier- [SOURCE: Page_190]

entiation will depend on the strength of these positive and negative eects. The next [SOURCE: Page_190]

section describes the experiment designed to compare the two market designs. [SOURCE: Page_190]

5.3 Experimental design Our experimental design captures the main characteristics of mature water markets [SOURCE: Page_190]

where agricultural users participate both in the water rights market and in the allocation [SOURCE: Page_190]

market. Each water right entitles its owner to a share of available seasonal water, which [SOURCE: Page_190]

varies stochastically (with a known distribution) and is only known with certainty at [SOURCE: Page_190]

a certain time of year (usually at the end of spring, when water levels in dams have [SOURCE: Page_190]

stabilized).   Water is used as an input in the agricultural production process with a [SOURCE: Page_190]

decreasing marginal productivity.      To mimic the relevant features of water markets [SOURCE: Page_190]

for the research question we wish to address, the experimental design simpli es the [SOURCE: Page_190]

market structure. Subjects trade water rights and water allocation in two successive [SOURCE: Page_190]

non overlapping phases. Water rights and allocation are only traded within a period [SOURCE: Page_190]

and not across periods. This choice precludes trading motives associated with long-term [SOURCE: Page_190]

strategies such as banking and speculation on the future value of water rights. It enables [SOURCE: Page_190]

us to observe trading strategies associated with the need to reduce transactions costs [SOURCE: Page_190]

and manage risk better, and to compare these strategies for a single security system [SOURCE: Page_190]

(which has only one level of security for shares) with a two security levels system (that [SOURCE: Page_190]

has two security levels for shares). [SOURCE: Page_190]

189 [SOURCE: Page_190]

To prevent prior attitudes about environmental policy from in uencing subjects' be- [SOURCE: Page_191]

havior, a neutral terminology is used: in particular, water rights are called shares and [SOURCE: Page_191]

water allocations are called  coupons . A share is thus an entitlement to a pre-speci ed [SOURCE: Page_191]

fraction of the total available coupons to be issued. At the end of each period, coupons [SOURCE: Page_191]

held are converted into ECU bene ts, the ECU being an experimental monetary unit [SOURCE: Page_191]

convertible at a xed rate into cash. [SOURCE: Page_191]

5.3.1 Treatments We use a 2x^2 factorial design with 6 observations per cell.             The treatment variables [SOURCE: Page_191]

are the number of levels of security for shares (1 or 2) and the presence or absence of [SOURCE: Page_191]

transactions costs (TC) in the share and coupon market.               We use a between subject [SOURCE: Page_191]

design where each subject participated in one of four treatments. [SOURCE: Page_191]

One level of security      Two security levels [SOURCE: Page_191]

TC in the coupon market only                 C$_1$                        C$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_191]

TC in the share market only                  S$_1$                        S$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_191]

The rst treatment dimension is the number of security levels. In C$_1$ and S$_1$, there is [SOURCE: Page_191]

only one type of shares called shares. In the two security levels treatment (C$_2$ and S$_2$), [SOURCE: Page_191]

high security (shares A) and low security shares (shares B) are traded sequentially, 4 with the high security shares traded rst. [SOURCE: Page_191]

The second treatment dimension is the transactions costs. Our rst set of treatments [SOURCE: Page_191]

(C$_1$ and C$_2$) follows Freebairn and Quiggin (2006) who suggest that seasonal alloca- [SOURCE: Page_191]

tion trading is likely to be associated with larger transaction costs. However Brennan [SOURCE: Page_191]

(2006) suggests that the nancial and administrative costs of allocation trade are rela- [SOURCE: Page_191]

tively small. Field interviews that we conducted in northern Victoria (Australia) also [SOURCE: Page_191]

largely provide evidence of higher transactions costs in the water rights market. This 5 constitutes our second set of treatments (S$_1$ and S$_2$).             This article mainly focuses on [SOURCE: Page_191]

the comparison of the rst treatment dimension (number of security levels). We use [SOURCE: Page_191]

4. In eld settings (for example in the Australian context), both markets could operate simulta- neously but the high security market tends to be more active.     Theoretically, the order of the two markets will not impact the equilibrium of both markets. Experimentally, some order eects may be observed. To limit the number of treatments, we choose to run the experiment with the high security share market rst then the low security market as it is more intuitive to trade rst the more secure assets. 5. We could also have run complementary and intermediary treatments with no transactions costs [SOURCE: Page_191]

190 [SOURCE: Page_191]

the second treatment dimension in order to test if our results are consistent with an [SOURCE: Page_192]

alternative con guration of transactions costs. This alternative is seen to be more em- [SOURCE: Page_192]

pirically relevant and hence improves the external validity of our results. We do not [SOURCE: Page_192]

directly compare C$_1$ with S$_1$ and C$_2$ with S$_2$ as these are just two dierent states of [SOURCE: Page_192]

the world where transactions costs are higher in one or the other market. In the ex- [SOURCE: Page_192]

periment, when one market entails low transaction costs, we normalize them to 0 (for [SOURCE: Page_192]

shares trading in C$_1$ and C$_2$ and coupons in S$_1$ and S$_2$). The higher transaction costs [SOURCE: Page_192]

are captured in the experiment by a fee of 2 ECUs per coupon (in C$_1$ and C$_2$) or per 6 share (in S$_1$ and S$_2$) traded, paid both by the buyer and by the seller. [SOURCE: Page_192]

5.3.2 Game structure Figure 5.1 presents the game structure. At the beginning of each period, each of the subject is endowed with an equal number of shares: 9 shares in the treatments with a [SOURCE: Page_192]

single level of security and 3 shares A (high security) and 12 shares B (low security) [SOURCE: Page_192]

in the treatments with two security levels. Each subject is also given an initial cash [SOURCE: Page_192]

amount of 50 ECUs which enables him to buy shares and coupons if he wishes. [SOURCE: Page_192]

In stage 1, subjects can choose to modify the number of shares they hold by buying [SOURCE: Page_192]

and selling in the share market. Between stage 1 and stage 2, a random draw selects [SOURCE: Page_192]

the scenario (blue or yellow, both of which are equally likely) that determines the [SOURCE: Page_192]

number of coupons subjects get from their shares (              Table 5.1 ). The scenarios are a simpli ed representation of the climatic variability. A wet season is described by the [SOURCE: Page_192]

 blue scenario whereas a dry season is called the  yellow scenario and corresponds [SOURCE: Page_192]

to three times less water available: 54 coupons are available in the blue scenario, and [SOURCE: Page_192]

18 coupons in the yellow scenario.          The succession of scenarios was randomly drawn [SOURCE: Page_192]

in advance and is identical across treatments and groups.               This ensures that we can [SOURCE: Page_192]

compare behavior across treatments keeping the climatic distribution constant.                     The [SOURCE: Page_192]

or equal transactions costs in both markets. However, under these con gurations of transactions costs, we can show that the incentives to trade shares are reduced, thus limiting the gains from a two security levels system. Due to budget limitations, we focus our data collection eorts on the two treatments for which the gains from a two security levels system are theoretically the highest (C$_1$-C$_2$) and the lowest (S$_1$-S$_2$). 6. This fee is high compared to the fee/water price ratio observed in operational water trading platforms. We chose to set a high transaction fee in the lab to capture all the non-monetary but time- consuming transactions costs born by farmers including writing contracts, locating and identifying trading partners. Moreover, it may be unusual for buyers and sellers to pay the same transaction cost although theoretically the burden of the cost should be shared equally if the market is competitive. 7. In one treatment of Godby et al. (1997), shares are kept from one period to the other (banking). This design feature could be relevant for water markets as water rights are equivalent to an asset yielding returns every season.   As this design places substantial cognitive demands on the subjects, Godby et al.     provided computerized advice on intertemporal optimization of shares and coupons holding. We want to avoid such complexity. Moreover, banking of shares is not necessary to observe the types of market gains we are interested in (transaction costs saving and better risk allocation). As a result, our design is simpler: each period starts with the same initial number of shares. [SOURCE: Page_194]

The share and coupon markets are organized according to continuous double auction [SOURCE: Page_195]

(CDA) rules in order to mimic the electronic clearing houses used by farmers to trade [SOURCE: Page_195]

water. Most of these trading platforms work on the basis of a continuous double auc- [SOURCE: Page_195]

tion (Productivity Comission (2010), Brooks and Harris (2008)). Moreover, CDA is a [SOURCE: Page_195]

useful mechanism in the lab because multiple trading opportunities are important in [SOURCE: Page_195]

experimental markets to improve e ciency (Cason and Friedman (2008)). Subjects can [SOURCE: Page_195]

place their price bids to buy extra shares or coupons, and/or price oers to sell them. [SOURCE: Page_195]

All these strategies, namely buy, sell and keep, can be pursued simultaneously. Each [SOURCE: Page_195]

trading stage is open for 2 minutes. [SOURCE: Page_195]

5.3.3 Subject types Subjects have marginal bene t functions parametrized to mimic two types of farmers [SOURCE: Page_195]

(   Table 5.2 ). Type 1 subjects' marginal bene ts mimic a mixed crop producer, with relatively low value for water and an elastic water demand. Type 2 subjects represent [SOURCE: Page_195]

farmers with high-value crops such as orchards or vineyard, who are highly sensitive [SOURCE: Page_195]

to irrigation restrictions. They need a minimum volume of irrigation water to preserve [SOURCE: Page_195]

the long term productivity of their plantations or to avoid catastrophic harvest losses. [SOURCE: Page_195]

Type 2 subjects therefore display a high marginal value for water, a rather inelastic [SOURCE: Page_195]

water demand, and a minimum water requirement.               The rst three coupons have no [SOURCE: Page_195]

value for a type 2 subject because they are insu cient to ensure production, but the [SOURCE: Page_195]

fourth coupon yields a high marginal value. In each market group, we randomly assign [SOURCE: Page_195]

marginal bene t functions to subjects so as to have three type-1 subjects and three [SOURCE: Page_195]

type-2 subjects. [SOURCE: Page_195]

194 [SOURCE: Page_195]

5.3.4 Experimental procedure The experiment was programmed and conducted at the University of Montpellier exper- [SOURCE: Page_196]

imental lab (LEEM) using the software z-Tree (Fischbacher (2007)). The subjects were [SOURCE: Page_196]

drawn from the undergraduate student population. Subjects interacted anonymously [SOURCE: Page_196]

in 6 person xed groups. For each treatment, we conducted 2 sessions of 3 groups each, [SOURCE: Page_196]

thus obtaining 6 independent observations per treatment, with a total of 144 subjects. [SOURCE: Page_196]

Each session lasted 3 hours. [SOURCE: Page_196]

At the beginning of each session, subjects participated in an individual lottery task that [SOURCE: Page_196]

helped us elicit their risk preferences (Brown and Stewart (1999)). The switch point [SOURCE: Page_196]

of this lottery task was used as a relative indicator of risk aversion.   In our sample, [SOURCE: Page_196]

57% of the subjects are risk averse (switch point from 1 to 4), 40% are risk neutral [SOURCE: Page_196]

(switch point of 5 or 6) and 3% risk lovers (switch point from 6 to 10). The random [SOURCE: Page_196]

assignment of subjects to types was successful as we do not observe any signi cant [SOURCE: Page_196]

correlation between type and elicited risk aversion (coe cient of correlation r= -0.0837; [SOURCE: Page_196]

p value=0.3437). This will enable us to compare the relative impact of type and risk [SOURCE: Page_196]

aversion on behavior in the game. [SOURCE: Page_196]

After the lottery task, subjects were invited to read the instructions of the experiment [SOURCE: Page_196]

explaining the dierent stages of the game, the trading software and the monetary [SOURCE: Page_196]

incentives. They also answered a quiz which tested their understanding of the game. [SOURCE: Page_196]

Subjects played two practice periods (with the same parameters as the rest of the [SOURCE: Page_196]

experiment), which did not count toward subjects' earnings, followed by a series of 9 [SOURCE: Page_196]

(C$_2$), 10 (S$_2$) and 12 (C$_1$-S$_1$) periods, one of which could potentially be selected for [SOURCE: Page_196]

payment. Subjects earned 18.50 euros on average and received an additional 1, 3.5 or [SOURCE: Page_196]

6 euros, depending on their choice and the outcome of the lottery task.       In order to [SOURCE: Page_196]

control for wealth eects, subjects' gains from the lottery were only revealed at the [SOURCE: Page_196]

end of the session. Qualitative and quantitative information was collected in the form [SOURCE: Page_196]

of questionnaires from the participants at the end of each session.       Instructions for [SOURCE: Page_196]

treatment C$_2$ are available in the appendix 5.A. [SOURCE: Page_196]

195 [SOURCE: Page_196]

5.4 Theoretical predictions This section presents the theoretical predictions on quantities and prices in the share [SOURCE: Page_197]

and the coupon markets in each treatment.                We rst solve the model under the risk [SOURCE: Page_197]

neutrality assumption and then present some intuition on the eect of risk aversion. We [SOURCE: Page_197]

solve the model for a two-agent market (with one agent of each type). For a market of 6 [SOURCE: Page_197]

participants (3 type-1 and 3 type-2), the price predictions are the same and the traded [SOURCE: Page_197]

quantities are simply multiplied by 3. The model is solved by backward induction: the [SOURCE: Page_197]

equilibrium of the coupon market is computed rst, then the equilibrium in the share [SOURCE: Page_197]

market is derived. In the experiment, share trading takes place rst, followed by coupon [SOURCE: Page_197]

trading once the scenario is drawn. [SOURCE: Page_197]

A risk neutral agent chooses the number of shares as well as the number of coupons in [SOURCE: Page_197]

order to maximize his net expected bene t from trading and coupon holding. P M ax Si ,ci,t   t=1,2 πt [B(ci,t ) + pc,t . (Wt .Si − ci,t ) − T c.dci,t . (Wt .Si − ci,t )] [SOURCE: Page_197]

+ps . (Qi − Si ) − T s.dsi . (Qi − Si ) Share: Qi is the initial allocation of shares to agent i, Si is the number of shares held in equilibrium, pS is the equilibrium price of a share. [SOURCE: Page_197]

Allocation of coupons: t indexes the scenario (t = 1, 2 for the two scenarios: yellow and blue), πt is the probability of occurrence of scenario t (with π1 + π2 = 1), Wt is the number of coupons received per share under scenario t. This value is known before the opening of the coupon market. [SOURCE: Page_197]

Coupon: ci,t is the number of coupons held by agent i in scenario t, B(ci,t ) is the marginal bene t function of agent i, which depends on the number of coupons held. Bi$_0$ (ci,t ) = ai − 2bi ci,t , atype^1 < atype^2 and btype^1 < btype^2 , pc,t is the equilibrium price of a coupon under scenario t. Transactions costs: T c is the transaction cost to buy and to sell in the coupon market, T s is the transaction cost to buy and to sell in the share market, dci,t is the net position of agent i in the coupon market in the scenario t and dsi in the share market. It is equal to 1 for a net seller, -1 for a net buyer. [SOURCE: Page_197]

We note here that the market for shares is unaected by the scenario. This is explained [SOURCE: Page_197]

in the section 5.4.2. [SOURCE: Page_197]

196 [SOURCE: Page_197]

5.4.1 Equilibrium in the coupon market Trading of coupons takes place until coupons' marginal bene ts, net of transaction [SOURCE: Page_198]

costs, are equal for the two agents.   Figure 5.2 represents the demand functions for coupons of type 1 and type 2 subjects. Equilibrium prices and quantities of coupons [SOURCE: Page_198]

held are found where total demand is equal to total supply in each scenario. Parameters [SOURCE: Page_198]

are chosen so that in equilibrium, type 1 subjects sell all their coupons to type 2 and [SOURCE: Page_198]

do not held coupons when they are scarce and expensive (in the yellow scenario). This [SOURCE: Page_198]

is because the equilibrium price is greater than the marginal value of even the rst [SOURCE: Page_198]

coupon held for type 1. The equilibrium price is between the minimum price type 1 is [SOURCE: Page_198]

willing to sell at and the maximum price type 2 is willing to buy at. The bargaining [SOURCE: Page_198]

power of each type in the game determine the equilibrium price. In treatments C$_1$ and [SOURCE: Page_198]

C$_2$ (compared to S$_1$ and S$_2$) in the blue scenario, the nal number of coupons held [SOURCE: Page_198]

is greater for a type 1 net seller (because he sells less in the presence of transactions [SOURCE: Page_198]

costs) and lower for a type 2 net buyer (because he buys less).   Table 5.4 summarizes the equilibrium prices and quantities in the coupon market in each scenario. [SOURCE: Page_198]

Figure 5.2: Equilibrium in the coupon market [SOURCE: Page_198]

Figure 5.2 presents the equilibrium for a 2-subjects market. For a 6-subjects market, the supply is multiplied by 3 and the equilibrium price and number of coupons held remain the same. Quantities traded are multiplied by 3. [SOURCE: Page_198]

197 [SOURCE: Page_198]

5.4.2 Equilibrium in the share market The maximum willingness to pay for the purchase of one share (or the minimum will- [SOURCE: Page_199]

ingness to accept for the sale of one share) is its expected value:               it is equal to the [SOURCE: Page_199]

expected number of coupons obtained from this share multiplied by the expected price [SOURCE: Page_199]

of coupons. The marginal bene ts of coupons have no impact on the willingness to pay [SOURCE: Page_199]

for shares because the coupon market plays the role of a reconciliation market: agents [SOURCE: Page_199]

can buy more or sell extra coupons in the coupon market. [SOURCE: Page_199]

In the absence of transactions costs in the coupon market (S$_1$ and S$_2$), the expected [SOURCE: Page_199]

value of a share is equal for all risk-neutral agents. As a result, no trade should take [SOURCE: Page_199]

place in the share market.         The presence of transactions costs in the share market [SOURCE: Page_199]

reinforces this result.    On the contrary, transactions costs in the coupon market (C$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_199]

and C$_2$) create heterogeneity in the expected value of a share across subjects if they [SOURCE: Page_199]

anticipate that they will have a dierent position (net buyer or net seller) in the coupon [SOURCE: Page_199]

market. As a result, trading of shares occurs in equilibrium in treatments C$_1$ and C$_2$. [SOURCE: Page_199]

The equilibrium price of a share is an interval, with the lower bound the minimum [SOURCE: Page_199]

price at which a net seller in the coupon market is willing to sell a share and the higher [SOURCE: Page_199]

bound being the maximum price at which a net buyer of coupons is willing to buy a [SOURCE: Page_199]

share: pS ∈ [E [W × (pc − T Fc )] ; E [W × (pc + T Fc .)]] (       see Table 5.4 ). Being net buyers in the coupon market, type 2 subjects are willing to pay more for [SOURCE: Page_199]

shares than type 1 subjects. As a result, type 1 will sell shares to type 2. The equi- [SOURCE: Page_199]

librium allocation of shares in C$_1$ and C$_2$ is such that the need for costly trade in the [SOURCE: Page_199]

coupon market is minimized. When only one level of security shares are available (C$_1$), [SOURCE: Page_199]

the equilibrium number of shares held by each subject is such that coupon trading is [SOURCE: Page_199]

required only in the yellow scenario. Each subject will hold shares such that it corre- [SOURCE: Page_199]

sponds to his needs for coupons in the blue scenario. A simple calculation shows that [SOURCE: Page_199]

any other allocation of shares is less e cient as it requires more trade in the coupon 8 market.       When two security levels for shares are available (C$_2$), the experiment is [SOURCE: Page_199]

8. Assume type 1 holds 6 shares. He will receive 2 shares in the yellow scenario and 6 shares in the blue scenario. He will have to sell 2 coupons if the scenario is yellow and 0 coupons if the scenario is blue. If he holds less than 6 shares, he will have to sell less than 2 coupons in the yellow scenario but will have to buy coupons in the blue scenario. He will on average pay more transactions costs to trade coupons. If he holds more than 6 shares, he will have to sell more than 2 coupons in the yellow scenario and will also have to sell coupons in the blue scenario. He will pay more transactions costs to trade coupons. The same reasoning applies for type 2 with 12 shares. Therefore, at equilibrium, each subject hold shares such that it corresponds to his needs for coupons in the blue scenario. [SOURCE: Page_199]

198 [SOURCE: Page_199]

parametrized such that, by constituting an e cient portfolio of shares, no trade is re- [SOURCE: Page_200]

quired in the coupon market in either scenario.     High security shares are bought to [SOURCE: Page_200]

cover the need for coupons in the yellow scenario. Low security shares are bought to [SOURCE: Page_200]

complement the allocation from high security shares in the blue scenario. [SOURCE: Page_200]

From the equilibrium predictions in the share market, one can compute the number of [SOURCE: Page_200]

coupons that will be received by each type in each treatment and scenario. This needs [SOURCE: Page_200]

to be compared to the equilibrium number of coupons held by each type to determine [SOURCE: Page_200]

the equilibrium number of trades in the coupon market. Equilibrium predictions for [SOURCE: Page_200]

the number of trades in each market are presented in    Table 5.4. [SOURCE: Page_200]

5.4.3 Impact of risk aversion In theory, there is no impact of risk aversion on the number of coupons held at the end [SOURCE: Page_200]

of the experiment since uncertainty is resolved when decisions on coupons are taken. [SOURCE: Page_200]

However, risk aversion potentially impacts the willingness of subjects to participate [SOURCE: Page_200]

in one market rather than the other. This eect is ambiguous when subjects can be [SOURCE: Page_200]

both buyers and sellers and can trade both in the coupon and the share market. Risk [SOURCE: Page_200]

aversion can potentially have two eects. On the one hand, risk averse subjects may [SOURCE: Page_200]

prefer to trade in the coupon market as more information is available at this stage. [SOURCE: Page_200]

Even if coupons trading is costly (treatments C$_1$-C$_2$), they may be willing to trade-o [SOURCE: Page_200]

greater transactions costs for a gain in information.   On the other hand, risk averse [SOURCE: Page_200]

subjects may be willing to buy shares as an insurance against a small allocation of [SOURCE: Page_200]

coupons (if the scenario is yellow), in order to secure a minimum number of coupons. [SOURCE: Page_200]

This is particularly true for type 2 subjects because they need at least 4 coupons to get [SOURCE: Page_200]

bene ts from coupons. As a result, if the rst (second) eect is stronger, the trading [SOURCE: Page_200]

activity in the share market is expected to be lower (higher) under risk aversion as [SOURCE: Page_200]

compared to the risk neutral prediction. The lack of clear theoretical predictions under [SOURCE: Page_200]

risk aversion reinforces the reason for conducting experiments. [SOURCE: Page_200]

199 [SOURCE: Page_200]

5.4.4 Pro ts Theoretical pro ts are dierent across treatments as they are dependent on transactions [SOURCE: Page_201]

costs (   Table 5.4 ). Under the risk neutrality assumption, pro ts are in theory equal in S$_1$ and S$_2$ because no trade is expected in the share market, thus no transactions [SOURCE: Page_201]

costs are paid in equilibrium.     Pro ts are on average higher in C$_2$ than C$_1$, because [SOURCE: Page_201]

the two security levels system oers the possibility to subjects to hold a portfolio of [SOURCE: Page_201]

shares which matches their needs for coupons in each scenario perfectly. Subjects can [SOURCE: Page_201]

thus avoid trading in the coupon market and therefore save transactions costs in C$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_201]

compared to C$_1$.      Table 5.4 presents equilibrium pro ts under the assumptions of risk neutrality and equal bargaining power of buyers and sellers (the latter assumption helps [SOURCE: Page_201]

avoid the problem of having interval predictions). [SOURCE: Page_201]

The variability of pro ts - measured by the dierence between equilibrium pro ts in [SOURCE: Page_201]

the blue scenario and pro ts in the yellow scenario - is dierent across types and across [SOURCE: Page_201]

treatments. By de nition of the variability of pro ts, it is lower for type 1 subjects as [SOURCE: Page_201]

they have lower pro ts in absolute value. The interesting prediction that arises however [SOURCE: Page_201]

is that the eect of treatment is type-dependent: type 2 has less variable gains in C$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_201]

than C$_1$ but it is the opposite for type 1. How can we relate this result to the e cient [SOURCE: Page_201]

risk sharing theory ?     E cient risk sharing theory suggests that agents should bear [SOURCE: Page_201]

a share of the risk proportional to their risk tolerance (Borch (1962), Wilson (1968), [SOURCE: Page_201]

Eeckhoudt et al. (2005)). An improved risk allocation decreases the variability of pro ts [SOURCE: Page_201]

for the less risk-tolerant and increases it for the more risk-tolerant (risk tolerance is equal [SOURCE: Page_201]

to the inverse of risk aversion). Type 2 subjects do not display signi cantly dierent [SOURCE: Page_201]

intrinsic risk aversion than type 1 (due to randomization), but they have a more concave [SOURCE: Page_201]

bene t function in the experiment. This may induce more reluctance to adopt risky [SOURCE: Page_201]

decisions by type 2 subjects, or more induced risk aversion. We assume that subjects' [SOURCE: Page_201]

behaviors are in uenced more by induced risk aversion (through type) than by intrinsic [SOURCE: Page_201]

risk aversion (ERA, elicited in the lottery game) (see Schoemaker (1993) for a review [SOURCE: Page_201]

of the dierence between elicited risk aversion and observed risk taking behavior). If [SOURCE: Page_201]

this assumption holds, an e cient allocation of risk is such that type 2 subjects, who [SOURCE: Page_201]

have lower induced risk tolerance, transfer part of their risk to type 1 subjects.          In [SOURCE: Page_201]

equilibrium, the allocation of risk is therefore improved in C$_2$ as compared to C$_1$. This [SOURCE: Page_201]

result does not hold when transactions costs are higher in the shares market (S$_2$-S$_1$) as [SOURCE: Page_201]

there is no dierence in the variability of pro ts between S$_1$ and S$_2$ . [SOURCE: Page_201]

200 [SOURCE: Page_201]

5.4.5 Hypotheses From the theoretical predictions discussed above, we draw the following hypotheses: [SOURCE: Page_202]

Hypothesis 1: A share system with two security levels increases overall pro ts when the transactions costs are higher on the coupon market (H1a).                 However, when it is [SOURCE: Page_202]

more costly to trade shares than to trade coupons, there are no pro t gains from a [SOURCE: Page_202]

system with two security levels (H1b). [SOURCE: Page_202]

Hypothesis 2: The two security level system improves risk management: the overall variability of pro ts is decreased (H2a) and risk allocation is improved (H2b). Since [SOURCE: Page_202]

the two security level system oers better options to subjects to adjust their portfolio [SOURCE: Page_202]

of rights to their preferences, more risk-averse subjects (type 2) will have less variable [SOURCE: Page_202]

pro ts and less risk-averse subjects (type 1) more variable pro ts.                 Hypothesis 2 [SOURCE: Page_202]

holds under the condition that trade occurs in the share market. Therefore, we expect [SOURCE: Page_202]

hypothesis 2 to be veri ed only in treatments C$_2$ vs C$_1$ (and not in S$_2$ vs S$_1$). [SOURCE: Page_202]

5.5 Results We compare the market performance of treatments C$_1$ and S$_1$ with treatments C$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_202]

and S$_2$ in terms of pro ts, e ciency and variability of pro ts in order to test our 9 hypotheses. [SOURCE: Page_202]

For each hypothesis, we rst present the results from the treatments for which the gains [SOURCE: Page_202]

of the two security level system are theoretically expected to be higher (C$_1$ and C$_2$). [SOURCE: Page_202]

Then, we present the results for the treatments that better re ect empirical reality [SOURCE: Page_202]

(S$_1$ and S$_2$) and examine how higher transaction costs in the share market impact the [SOURCE: Page_202]

performance of the two security level system. [SOURCE: Page_202]

We examine the dierences across treatments using nonparametric Mann-Whitney U [SOURCE: Page_202]

tests with exactly one summary statistic value for each of the six independent groups [SOURCE: Page_202]

in each treatment. We present the p-values of the one-sided tests in most comparisons [SOURCE: Page_202]

as it provides more power to detect an eect when there is a speci c hypothesis about [SOURCE: Page_202]

the direction of the eect.       When relevant, we also report results from multivariate [SOURCE: Page_202]

9. Pro ts are a function of market prices and quantities traded, which are presented in Table 5.6. However, in this article, we do not analyze market prices and quantities traded and focus on aggregate results in order to provide a clearer message in terms of comparison of policy options. [SOURCE: Page_202]

201 [SOURCE: Page_202]

regression models which evaluate the contribution of dierent factors on the decisions [SOURCE: Page_203]

made by subjects. Unless speci ed, we compute the statistics for the last four periods of [SOURCE: Page_203]

the experiment common to all the treatments (periods 6 to 9), as we are interested in the [SOURCE: Page_203]

performance in the later part of the sessions after an initial learning and equilibration [SOURCE: Page_203]

phase. The last four periods include 2 blue periods (7 and 8) and 2 yellow periods (6 [SOURCE: Page_203]

and 9). The results show similar patterns when all periods are considered. [SOURCE: Page_203]

5.5.1 Hypothesis 1: Pro ts Hypothesis H1a is supported by the experimental results:       when transactions costs [SOURCE: Page_203]

are greater in the coupon market, average pro ts are signi cantly higher under a two [SOURCE: Page_203]

security level system (C$_2$ compared to C$_1$,    Table 5.5 ). The lower number of coupons traded and therefore the lower transactions costs paid in C$_2$ compared to C$_1$ explain [SOURCE: Page_203]

Figure 5.3 and Table 5.6 ). Theory predicts that in equilibrium no trade this result ( [SOURCE: Page_203]

of coupons should occur in C$_2$.     In the experiment, 39.6% of subjects in C$_2$ reach [SOURCE: Page_203]

the equilibrium portfolio of shares and hence do not have to trade coupons and pay [SOURCE: Page_203]

transactions costs. [SOURCE: Page_203]

Figure 5.3: Transactions costs paid by each subject [SOURCE: Page_203]

202 [SOURCE: Page_203]

We can measure the share of the potential gain realized by using an e ciency ratio. The [SOURCE: Page_204]

max-gain e ciency ratio is de ned as the observed pro ts over the maximum attainable [SOURCE: Page_204]

pro ts (equilibrium prediction assuming equal bargaining power of the subjects). Max- [SOURCE: Page_204]

gain e ciency ratios are high in all treatments (         Table 5.5 ). This is partly due to the parameters of the experiment: if agents do not trade at all during the experiment (they [SOURCE: Page_204]

keep their initial allocation of shares and coupons), they can reach an average e ciency [SOURCE: Page_204]

level of more than 85%. We nevertheless observe dierences across treatments: the max- [SOURCE: Page_204]

gain e ciency ratios are on average lower in C$_1$ than C$_2$. We also compute the no-trade [SOURCE: Page_204]

e ciency ratio - de ned as the observed pro ts over the pro ts if subjects do not trade [SOURCE: Page_204]

at all - for each treatment. The no-trade-e ciency ratios are higher than 100%, which [SOURCE: Page_204]

indicates that subjects increase their pro ts by trading in the dierent markets, even if [SOURCE: Page_204]

they do not reach the maximum potential gains (as de ned by the max-gain e ciency [SOURCE: Page_204]

ratio). The no-trade-e ciency ratios are also on average signi cantly lower in C$_1$ than [SOURCE: Page_204]

C$_2$. These results suggest that the complexity of the two security level market is not 10 detrimental to e ciency. [SOURCE: Page_204]

Pro ts are theoretically identical under treatments S$_1$ and S$_2$, but in the experiment, [SOURCE: Page_204]

we observe that pro ts are signi cantly lower in S$_2$ (          Table 5.5 ). This can be explained by the fact that subjects fail to reach the predicted no-trade equilibrium in the share [SOURCE: Page_204]

market: only one quarter of the subjects do not trade shares (in S$_1$, 29.9% do not trade [SOURCE: Page_204]

any shares; in S$_2$, 25.6% do not trade low security shares and 25% do not trade high [SOURCE: Page_204]

security shares). However, there is evidence that subjects learn to approach the no-trade [SOURCE: Page_204]

equilibrium as the transactions costs paid in S$_1$ and S$_2$ decrease over time (             Figure 5.3 ). 10. We mentioned in section 5.2 that the increased complexity of water markets with dierent types of water rights may reduce farmers' participation in the market. In the lab, we may therefore nd that the complexity of treatments C$_2$ and S$_2$ could lead to less trades and hence lower e ciency. To examine this, we de ne the deviation from the e cient portfolio of shares (the one that minimizes their needs to buy and sell coupons in stage 2) and coupons (the one that maximizes their gains) as a measure of the eect of complexity on the market performance of each treatment. The complexity of a two security levels system does not have any impact on the capacity of subjects to reach the equilibrium portfolio of shares and coupons as they do not deviate more in the two security levels treatments (C$_2$ and S$_2$) as compared to the single-security treatments (C$_1$ and S$_1$). In addition, we also estimated random eects generalized least squares regression models with clustering, where the dependent variable is the dierence (in absolute terms) between the observed variable (number of coupons received and number of coupons held) and the theoretical prediction. Consistent with the results from the other tests, the treatment dummy has no signi cant impact. These results are not presented here but they suggest that subjects did not nd the trading setup in C$_2$ and S$_2$ more complex.         We tried to ensure that complexity or confusion was reduced to the extent possible, for example subjects participated in a quiz after the instructions were read out to ensure that they understood the experiment. In the post experimental questionnaire, subjects did not indicate any confusion with any aspect of the experiment. [SOURCE: Page_204]

203 [SOURCE: Page_204]

The failure to reach the no-trade equilibrium could be the result of an experimental [SOURCE: Page_205]

demand eect, which is higher in S$_2$ than S$_1$, since there are two opportunities to [SOURCE: Page_205]

trade shares in S$_2$ (Zizzo (2010)). As a consequence, the number of shares traded (as [SOURCE: Page_205]

a sum of high and low security shares) and the total transaction costs paid in a group [SOURCE: Page_205]

are signi cantly higher in S$_2$ than S$_1$ (       Table 5.6 ). Max-gain e ciency and no-trade e ciency are reduced in S$_2$ as compared to S$_1$ because subjects trade more shares than [SOURCE: Page_205]

necessary. This leads to the lower performance of the two security levels treatment in [SOURCE: Page_205]

terms of average pro ts when there are higher transactions costs associated with share [SOURCE: Page_205]

trading. Hypothesis H1b (equality of pro ts in S$_1$ and S$_2$) is thus not supported by our 11 data. [SOURCE: Page_205]

Table 5.7 con rms these results with random eects generalized least squares regres- sions, where the dependent variable is the pro t made at the individual level in periods [SOURCE: Page_205]

1 to 9. Errors are clustered at the group level to capture any unobserved heterogeneity [SOURCE: Page_205]

in the group.     Two separate regressions are run for C$_1$-C$_2$ and S$_1$-S$_2$.              Explanatory [SOURCE: Page_205]

variables are treatment dummies, scenario dummies, type dummies as well as period [SOURCE: Page_205]

and elicited risk aversion (ERA). As expected, pro ts are lower under the yellow sce- [SOURCE: Page_205]

nario and for type 1 subjects. In the rst regression, the parameter of the treatment [SOURCE: Page_205]

dummy (C$_2$) is signi cant and positive, con rming the non parametric test results that [SOURCE: Page_205]

pro ts are higher under C$_2$ (thus providing additional support for H1a). In the second [SOURCE: Page_205]

regression, the treatment dummy (S$_2$) is negative and signi cant. H1b can therefore be [SOURCE: Page_205]

rejected. We also observe a signi cant and positive eect of period, revealing a learning [SOURCE: Page_205]

eect. The elicited risk aversion (ERA) is not signi cant. [SOURCE: Page_205]

11. This could partly be due to a design feature in our experiment that introduces the high and low security share markets sequentially. With simultaneous trading of high and low security shares, we may not observe more share trading in the two security levels treatment as compared to the treatment with a single type of shares. [SOURCE: Page_205]

204 [SOURCE: Page_205]

5.5.2 Hypothesis 2: Risk management Table 5.5 shows that the standard deviation of pro ts (which can be interpreted as a proxy for overall risk) is not signi cantly dierent across treatments. This initial test [SOURCE: Page_206]

invalidates hypothesis 2a which postulated that overall risk could be reduced with a [SOURCE: Page_206]

two security levels system. [SOURCE: Page_206]

Even though a two security levels system does not seem to reduce overall risk, data [SOURCE: Page_206]

show that it helps to share risk more e ciently. We argued before that the shape of the [SOURCE: Page_206]

bene t functions suggests that type 2 subjects may display a greater induced risk- [SOURCE: Page_206]

aversion than type 1 subjects. Therefore type 1 subjects are expected to favor trading [SOURCE: Page_206]

decisions that may increase the variability of their pro ts but increase their average [SOURCE: Page_206]

pro ts, whereas type 2 subjects are expected to take decisions which contribute to a [SOURCE: Page_206]

reduction in the variability of their pro ts, even if this reduction of risk has a cost in [SOURCE: Page_206]

terms of lower average pro ts. [SOURCE: Page_206]

Table 5.5 shows that the standard deviation of pro t is indeed signi cantly greater in C$_2$ than in C$_1$ for type 1 and lower in C$_2$ than C$_1$ for type 2. Contrary to theoretical [SOURCE: Page_206]

predictions, the same results hold for S$_1$ and S$_2$. A two security level system therefore [SOURCE: Page_206]

leads to an increase in the risk for type 1 and to a decrease for type 2 irrespective of [SOURCE: Page_206]

the transactions costs.   Table 5.8 presents regressions of the dierence between average pro ts in the blue scenario and average pro ts in the yellow scenario for each subject [SOURCE: Page_206]

type in each treatment comparison. The treatment variable (C$_2$ and S$_2$) has a positive [SOURCE: Page_206]

sign for type 1 (statistically signi cant for C$_2$ and marginally signi cant for S$_2$) and [SOURCE: Page_206]

negative sign for type 2. While the statistical signi cance is not uniformly strong in [SOURCE: Page_206]

Table 5.5 and Table 5.8, the direction of the eect is clear, hence providing some support for hypothesis 2b. Elicited risk aversion mostly does not have an impact (except for [SOURCE: Page_206]

type 1 in S$_1$-S$_2$) and this indicates that type is more relevant than elicited risk aversion [SOURCE: Page_206]

in explaining variability of pro ts. [SOURCE: Page_206]

The results concerning risk allocation are similar whatever the con guration of trans- [SOURCE: Page_206]

actions costs. It suggests that a two security levels system does not reduce risk but it [SOURCE: Page_206]

can improve risk allocation if the share market is active (even if there are no gains from [SOURCE: Page_206]

trading shares as in S$_2$). [SOURCE: Page_206]

Table 5.3 summarizes all the experimental results and compares them to the theoretical predictions. [SOURCE: Page_206]

205 [SOURCE: Page_206]

Table 5.3: Summary of results [SOURCE: Page_207]

Hypotheses                                             C$_2$ compared to C$_1$     S$_2$ compared to S$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_207]

H1a: Increased Pro ts                                    supported ++ H1b: Equal Pro ts                                                              not supported [SOURCE: Page_207]

H2a: Decreased Variability of Pro ts                     not supported         not supported H2b: Improved Risk Allocation between Types             some support +        some support + [SOURCE: Page_207]

++ supported by both non-parametric and parametric analysis + in the expected direction but not always statistically signi cant [SOURCE: Page_207]

5.6 Conclusion There is a major impetus for water reforms around the world. Much is expected from [SOURCE: Page_207]

the development of sophisticated water markets to improve the economic e ciency of [SOURCE: Page_207]

water allocation, especially in times of increasing scarcity and variability. In this article [SOURCE: Page_207]

we focus on the design of markets for water rights by analyzing the relative bene ts [SOURCE: Page_207]

of having rights with dierent levels of security.      This research can provide the rst [SOURCE: Page_207]

step towards designing water markets that can simultaneously achieve an e cient and [SOURCE: Page_207]

cost-eective allocation of water and risk.        While there are on-going policy debates [SOURCE: Page_207]

for improving the risk management potential of water markets, no previously reported [SOURCE: Page_207]

laboratory experiment has studied the impact of introducing water rights with dierent [SOURCE: Page_207]

security levels.   We show in this article that security-dierentiated water rights can [SOURCE: Page_207]

improve the performance of water markets but the outcome is also dependent on market [SOURCE: Page_207]

transactions costs. [SOURCE: Page_207]

Our results suggest that being allowed to hold and trade water rights with dierent [SOURCE: Page_207]

levels of security increases water users' pro ts, provided that transactions costs in the [SOURCE: Page_207]

rights market are lower than in the allocation market (treatments C$_1$ and C$_2$).              If [SOURCE: Page_207]

instead the allocation market is e cient and trading in this market is costless whereas [SOURCE: Page_207]

trading rights is costly, the pro ts from trading rights are reduced, as well as the gains [SOURCE: Page_207]

from a dierentiated water rights market. This system oers nevertheless interesting [SOURCE: Page_207]

206 [SOURCE: Page_207]

opportunities in terms of risk allocation, irrespective of the transactions cost scenario. [SOURCE: Page_208]

Our ndings indicate that there is a trade-o between water allocation and risk allo- [SOURCE: Page_208]

cation when trading rights is more costly as suggested by empirical evidence. In the [SOURCE: Page_208]

absence of perfect information about the preferences of farmers and water managers [SOURCE: Page_208]

with respect to these two objectives (e cient water allocation and risk allocation), the [SOURCE: Page_208]

potential bene ts of dierentiated markets for water rights cannot be overlooked. As [SOURCE: Page_208]

risk becomes a major concern for farmers, dierentiated markets may become a valu- [SOURCE: Page_208]

able water policy option as they can improve the allocation of risk by decreasing the [SOURCE: Page_208]

variability of pro ts for less risk-tolerant water users. This analysis does not include the [SOURCE: Page_208]

other bene ts of creating a water right system with several security levels: for example, [SOURCE: Page_208]

since water rights are permanent assets, whose value depends on the level of security [SOURCE: Page_208]

attached to them, farmers may be willing to hold high security rights in order to im- [SOURCE: Page_208]

prove the management of their long-term risks. These arguments reinforce our case in [SOURCE: Page_208]

favor of a dierentiated right system. Another policy recommendation is to ensure that [SOURCE: Page_208]

transactions costs on the market for water rights be minimized so that participants can [SOURCE: Page_208]

take the full advantage of the rights dierentiation. [SOURCE: Page_208]

Alternative mechanisms, such as option markets, have been proposed by policy makers [SOURCE: Page_208]

in order to improve the tools available to farmers to hedge the risk of water availability. [SOURCE: Page_208]

A water user can buy and option and pay a premium for the right to purchase water at [SOURCE: Page_208]

a later date, contingent on the pre-speci ed strike price (Howitt (1998), Hansen et al. [SOURCE: Page_208]

(2008)). Future research in this area could compare both policies using experimental [SOURCE: Page_208]

methods:   water rights markets to trade rights with dierent level of security or an [SOURCE: Page_208]

option market for future allocation. In order to compare these two alternative systems, [SOURCE: Page_208]

the trade-o between potential e ciency of the scheme and the necessary level of par- [SOURCE: Page_208]

ticipation in the markets would need to be considered. For example, it is possible that [SOURCE: Page_208]

the performance of a future market may be less dependent on the number of trades [SOURCE: Page_208]

and transactions costs as compared to a dierentiated water market. Further research [SOURCE: Page_208]

could also include eld experiments with farmers trained in water trading to examine [SOURCE: Page_208]

if farmers can take better advantage of a two security levels system than subjects in a [SOURCE: Page_208]

laboratory (Herberich et al. (2009)). [SOURCE: Page_208]

Appendix 5.A: Experimental instructions [SOURCE: Page_214]

The instructions reported are for treatment C$_2$. Instructions were modi ed slightly for the other treatments depending on the transaction costs scenario and the type and return of shares. Instructions are translated from French by native French speakers who are [SOURCE: Page_214]

uent in English. The text in italics has been added to make the instructions clearer for the reader, the participants did not see this. As a participant in this experiment, you will be asked to make decisions using a com- puter. This document gives you the instructions for the experiment. Please make sure you understand them correctly. This experiment has two parts You'll get the instruc- tions of the second part after all of you have completed the rst part. You will be paid according to your decisions for both parts. The computer will calculate your gains at the end of the experiment. They will be paid to you privately in cash at the end of the experiment. [SOURCE: Page_214]

Part 1: Risk aversion elicitation [SOURCE: Page_214]

For each of the 10 choices, you have to choose your preferred option between X and Y. The gain is xed for option X. There are two possible outcomes for option Y. The realized outcome will depend on the random draw of a number.        One of you will be chosen by the experimenter to draw a ball from a bag with the balls numbered from 1 to 10. [SOURCE: Page_214]

Your gain in Euros for part 1 depends on your choice and the number drawn. [SOURCE: Page_214]

213 [SOURCE: Page_214]

Option X          Option Y Payo    Number     Payo   Number Choice 1     3.5      1-10        1       1-9 6        10 Choice 2     3.5      1-10        1       1-8 6       9-10 Choice 3     3.5      1-10        1       1-7 6       8-10 Choice 4     3.5      1-10        1       1-6 6       7-10 Choice 5     3.5      1-10        1       1-5 6       6-10 Choice 6     3.5      1-10        1       1-4 6       5-10 Choice 7     3.5      1-10        1       1-3 6       4-10 Choice 8     3.5      1-10        1       1-2 6       3-10 Choice 9     3.5      1-10        1        1 6       2-10 Choice 10    3.5      1-10        1        0 6       1-10 [SOURCE: Page_215]

Part 2 [SOURCE: Page_215]

General principles of the experiment [SOURCE: Page_215]

The server computer will form randomly 3 groups of 6 participants. You will be part of one group, xed during the experiment. You can't identify the other members of your group and they can't identify you. [SOURCE: Page_215]

In this experiment, you will have the opportunity to realize transactions (buying or selling) of dierent goods: shares A, shares B and coupons.    All transactions will be realized in ECUs. [SOURCE: Page_215]

You can only trade with participants of your own group. [SOURCE: Page_215]

You will play over a number of periods. You will not learn the number of periods until the end of the experiment. [SOURCE: Page_215]

The same rules apply to each period: You are endowed with an initial number of shares and an initial cash endowment of 50 ECUs.        In stage 1, you can buy or sell shares [SOURCE: Page_215]

214 [SOURCE: Page_215]
